Section <a href="#sec:certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificate">4</a> states the universal algebraic estimate, and Section <a href="#sec:flux" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:flux">[sec:flux]</a> derives the pointwise geometric comparison from it. Section <a href="#sec:certificate-proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificate-proof">6</a> gives the complete exact verification; Section <a href="#sec:cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cutoffs">[sec:cutoffs]</a> then removes the cutoffs to obtain the contradiction. Section <a href="#sec:consequences" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:consequences">8</a> gives the regularity and sharpness consequences. Appendix <a href="#app:tables" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:tables">9</a> gives all coefficient data. All tensor norms and contractions use orthonormal frames. Repeated indices are summed, $\Delta=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nabla^2$, and a boundary normal denoted by $N$ points into the positive phase. The curvature $H=-T(N,N)$ used below is not divided by the dimension of the boundary.

<a id="red:section"></a>
### Reduction to a smooth link and its suspension

<span id="sec:reductions" label="sec:reductions"></span>

We first record the consequences of energy minimality needed to put the argument on a smooth spherical domain. Throughout Section <a href="#sec:reductions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reductions">[sec:reductions]</a>, a minimizer has the full ballwise Sobolev comparison property defined in Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>.

<a id="red:regularity-inputs"></a>
#### The regularity inputs

The ballwise convention also gives comparison on every bounded open set $O\Subset\mathbb R^d$: extend an $H^1_0(O)$ competitor difference by zero into a ball containing $\overline O$, apply minimality there, and cancel the energy outside $O$. Conversely, comparison on bounded open sets includes comparison on balls. It is even enough to have comparison against *arbitrary* admissible competitors in every origin-centered ball, by the same extension argument. There is no restriction to homogeneous competitors.

The convention that competitors are nonnegative makes no difference to the standard one-phase results. If $v-u\in H^1_0(O)$ and $u\geq0$, then $v_+-u\in H^1_0(O)$ and $$\int_O\bigl(|\nabla v_+|^2+\mathbf 1_{\{v_+>0\}}\bigr)
 \leq
 \int_O\bigl(|\nabla v|^2+\mathbf 1_{\{v>0\}}\bigr).$$ For completeness, the assertion about $H^1_0(O)$ follows by approximating $v-u$ in $H^1$ by compactly supported smooth functions and applying the continuity of positive-part truncation in $H^1$; the truncated differences in the approximation have compact support in $O$.

<a id="stmt-red-regularity"></a>
<div id="red:regularity" class="proposition">

**Proposition 3** (Regularity and blowups of minimizers). *Let $d\geq2$ and let $u\geq0$ be a local minimizer of the one-phase energy with volume coefficient $1$ in an open set $D\subset\mathbb R^d$.*

1.  *There is a locally Lipschitz representative of $u$, and it is harmonic in $\{u>0\}$.*

<a id="red:blowup-sequence"></a>
2.  *If $x_0\in D\cap\partial\{u>0\}$, every sequence $r_j\downarrow0$ has a subsequence for which $$\label{red:blowup-sequence}
     u_{x_0,r_j}(y):=\frac{u(x_0+r_jy)}{r_j}\longrightarrow U(y)$$ locally uniformly in $\mathbb R^d$. The limit is a nonzero, nonnegative, one-homogeneous global minimizer. Along a further subsequence the convergence is strong in $H^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$, and the positivity indicators converge in $L^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$.*

<a id="red:bernoulli"></a>
3.  *If one such limit equals $(y\cdot e)_+$ with $|e|=1$, then near $x_0$ the free boundary is a smooth hypersurface and $\{u>0\}$ is exactly one of its sides. The function $u$ is smooth up to that hypersurface from the positive side, and $$\label{red:bernoulli}
     u=0,\qquad \nabla u=N_E
     \quad\hbox{on the free boundary},$$ where $N_E$ is the Euclidean unit normal pointing into $\{u>0\}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-regularity"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-regularity</summary>

*Sources and applicability.* We use the constant-coefficient theory with $\Lambda=1$ in <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Velichkov2023](#ref-Velichkov2023)]. For each ball $B\Subset D$, the restriction of $u$ to $B$ minimizes against every $H^1_0(B)$ competitor difference. Zero extension of differences therefore gives comparison on every $O\Subset B$, as required by Definitions 2.11–2.12 there. We apply the local results inside such balls. For global minimizers, the containing-ball argument above gives the comparison convention on all bounded open sets. Local Lipschitz continuity is Theorem 3.1. Harmonicity also follows directly: for a smooth test supported compactly in the positive phase, both signs of a sufficiently small additive variation preserve that phase, so the Dirichlet first variation vanishes.

The Lipschitz bound and $u(x_0)=0$ give locally uniform compactness of the rescalings. Proposition 4.1 gives $\sup_{B_r(x_0)}u\geq\kappa r$ for small $r$, with $\kappa>0$, so a limit cannot vanish identically. Proposition 6.2 gives strong Sobolev and positivity-indicator convergence and the full minimizing property of the limit; Proposition 9.12 gives its one-homogeneity. All these statements can be applied in a bounded ball compactly contained in $D$ before taking the rescalings. In particular, the assertion about minimality is the minimizing compactness result, including the volume term.

Definition 6.10 and Corollary 8.2, with the one-sided graph conclusion in Theorem 8.1, give a $C^{1,\alpha}$ regular neighborhood from a single half-space blowup. Proposition 7.1 supplies the viscosity Bernoulli condition.

To justify all subsequent boundary derivatives, use <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[DeSilvaFerrariSalsa2019](#ref-DeSilvaFerrariSalsa2019), Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2] with $f_+=f_-=0$ and $G(b)=1+b$. We check the two-phase viscosity condition of <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[DeSilvaFerrariSalsa2014](#ref-DeSilvaFerrariSalsa2014), Definition 7.1] when $u\geq0$. Let $v$ be a comparison touching $u$ at a free-boundary point $x_0$, with a $C^2$ interface and $C^2$ branches having nonnegative normal slopes $a$ and $b$ on its positive and negative sides. If $v$ touches from below, then $v_+\leq u$ in a neighborhood. For $a>0$, extend its positive branch to a signed $C^2$ function $\phi$, negative just across the interface; then $\phi\leq u$ and touches at $x_0$. The one-phase condition gives $a\leq1\leq G(b)$; the case $a=0$ is immediate. If $v$ touches from above, the neighborhood inequality $v\geq u\geq0$ forces $v=u=0$ throughout its nonpositive side, so $b=0$. A $C^2$ extension $\phi$ of its positive branch satisfies $\phi_+\geq u$ on both sides and touches at $x_0$. Thus the one-phase condition gives $a\geq1=G(0)$. These uses of the one-phase condition are justified by <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Velichkov2023](#ref-Velichkov2023), Definition 7.6 and Proposition 7.1]: to meet its smooth-test convention, replace each $C^2$ extension by its quadratic Taylor polynomial minus, respectively plus, $\varepsilon|x-x_0|^2$. For any $\varepsilon>0$, these are smooth lower and upper supports in a sufficiently small neighborhood, with the same contact value and gradient.

The interior equations hold because $u$ is harmonic where positive and identically zero on the interior of its zero set. The function $G$ is smooth, strictly increasing and unbounded, with $G(0)=1$; the cited results allow the negative phase to vanish. The preceding $C^{1,\alpha}$ one-sided graph satisfies the slab flatness hypothesis after rotation and sufficiently small dilation, while the local Lipschitz bound is preserved. Theorem 1.1 gives $C^{2,\gamma}$ regularity, and Corollary 1.2 gives smoothness. Local harmonic boundary regularity with zero Dirichlet data <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[Hunter2014](#ref-Hunter2014), Theorems 4.30–4.31 and Corollary 4.32] then gives smoothness of $u$ from the positive side. These estimates apply after flattening a smooth boundary patch and localizing away from its artificial boundary; iteration and Sobolev embedding give every required derivative. The Bernoulli condition and nonnegativity give <a href="#red:bernoulli" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:bernoulli">[red:bernoulli]</a>. ◻

</details>

As in Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>, a free-boundary point is regular if it has a half-space blowup and singular otherwise. Proposition <a href="#red:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:regularity">3</a> explains the geometric meaning of regularity. Conversely, a smooth one-sided free boundary with <a href="#red:bernoulli" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:bernoulli">[red:bernoulli]</a> has a half-space blowup by its first-order Taylor expansion. We henceforth use the continuous representative of a minimizer. If it is one-homogeneous almost everywhere, it is one-homogeneous everywhere: for each fixed $r>0$, both sides of $u(rx)=ru(x)$ are continuous and agree almost everywhere.

<a id="red:dimension-reduction"></a>
#### Dimension reduction at a nonzero point

The next two lemmas give the dimension-reduction steps explicitly; compare <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Velichkov2023](#ref-Velichkov2023), Lemmas 10.9–10.10].

<a id="stmt-red-translation"></a>
<div id="red:translation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 4** (Translation invariance of an off-origin blowup). *Let $u$ be a one-homogeneous global minimizer, let $0\ne x_0\in\partial\{u>0\}$, and let $U$ be any limit in <a href="#red:blowup-sequence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:blowup-sequence">[red:blowup-sequence]</a>. Then $$U(y+t x_0)=U(y)\qquad(y\in\mathbb R^d,\ t\in\mathbb R).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-translation"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-translation</summary>

<a id="red:rescaling-identity"></a>
*Proof.* Write $u_r=u_{x_0,r}$. For fixed $t\in\mathbb R$ and small $r>0$, set $r'=r/(1+rt)>0$. Homogeneity gives the exact identity $$\label{red:rescaling-identity}
 u_r(y+t x_0)
 =\frac{u\bigl((1+rt)(x_0+r'y)\bigr)}r
 =u_{r'}(y).$$ Let $L$ be a Lipschitz constant for $u$ on a fixed neighborhood of $x_0$. Since $u(x_0)=0$, for $|y|\leq M$ and small $r$, $$\begin{aligned}
 |u_{r'}(y)-u_r(y)|
 &\leq\frac{L|r-r'|M}{r'}
       +LrM\left|\frac1{r'}-\frac1r\right|\\
 &=2LM\frac{|r-r'|}{r'}=2LM|t|r.
\end{aligned}$$ Thus the $r'$ rescalings along the same chosen sequence have the same locally uniform limit as the $r$ rescalings. Passing to that limit in <a href="#red:rescaling-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:rescaling-identity">[red:rescaling-identity]</a> proves the assertion for every fixed $y,t$. No uniqueness of blowups is required. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-red-factor-minimality"></a>
<div id="red:factor-minimality" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Minimality of a translation-invariant factor). *Suppose $U(y,s)=v(y)$ is a global minimizer in $\mathbb R^k\times\mathbb R$, where $v\geq0$ belongs to $H^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb R^k)$. Then $v$ is a global minimizer in $\mathbb R^k$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-factor-minimality"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-factor-minimality</summary>

*Proof.* Suppose that a ball $B\subset\mathbb R^k$ admits a nonnegative $q\in H^1(B)$ with $q-v\in H^1_0(B)$ and $$J(v;B)-J(q;B)=\delta>0.$$ Let $\psi$ be the zero extension of $q-v$. For $L>1$, choose $\eta_L:\mathbb R\to[0,1]$ equal to $1$ on $[-L,L]$, equal to $0$ outside $[-L-1,L+1]$, and linear on each intervening interval. Define $$V_L(y,s)=U(y,s)+\eta_L(s)\psi(y).$$ This function is nonnegative: on $B\times\mathbb R$ it is the convex combination $(1-\eta_L)v+\eta_Lq$. Its difference from $U$ belongs to $H^1$ and has compact support in a sufficiently large ball $\mathcal B_L\subset\mathbb R^{k+1}$, so it belongs to $H^1_0(\mathcal B_L)$.

The energy difference on $B\times[-L,L]$ is $-2L\delta$. On either transition cylinder, convexity gives $$|\nabla_y V_L|^2\leq(1-\eta_L)|\nabla v|^2+\eta_L|\nabla q|^2,
 \qquad |\partial_sV_L|^2\leq|q-v|^2.$$ The difference of the positivity indicators is at most $1$. Hence the sum of the two transition contributions is bounded above by $$C=2\int_B\bigl(|\nabla q|^2+|q-v|^2+1\bigr),$$ independently of $L$. Outside these three cylinders the energy is unchanged. Thus $$J(V_L;\mathcal B_L)-J(U;\mathcal B_L)\leq-2L\delta+C<0$$ for large $L$, contradicting minimality of $U$. ◻

</details>

In dimension one, every nonnegative one-homogeneous Sobolev function has the form $u(x)=b_+x_++b_-(-x)_+$ with $b_+,b_-\ge0$. If both slopes are positive, a nonnegative smooth bump $\phi$ supported in $(-R,R)$ with $\phi(0)>0$ preserves positivity volume and gives $$J(u+\tau\phi;(-R,R))-J(u;(-R,R))
 =-2\tau(b_++b_-)\phi(0)+\tau^2\int_{-R}^R|\phi'|^2<0$$ for small $\tau>0$. Thus a nonzero minimizing cone has just one positive slope. After reflection write $u(x)=b x_+$, $b>0$. The competitors $v_t(x)=bR(x-t)_+/(R-t)$ on $(-R,R)$, $|t|<R$, have the same trace and energy $b^2R^2/(R-t)+(R-t)$. Its derivative at the minimizing parameter $t=0$ is $b^2-1=0$, so $b=1$. This proves flatness in dimension one directly.

<a id="red:first-dimension"></a>
The lower-dimensional classification of Jerison–Savin <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[JerisonSavin2015](#ref-JerisonSavin2015), Theorem 1.1 and Corollary 1.2] and the dimension-seven minimizing example of De Silva–Jerison <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[DeSilvaJerison2009](#ref-DeSilvaJerison2009), Theorem 1.1] give $5\leq d_*\leq7$. De Silva–Jerison’s Theorem 1.1 uses the same functional and arbitrary ballwise Dirichlet competitors. For the exclusion argument, suppose from now on that $$\label{red:first-dimension}
 d=d_*\in\{5,6\},$$ and fix a nonzero nonflat one-homogeneous global minimizer $u$ in $\mathbb R^d$.

<a id="stmt-red-original-link"></a>
<div id="red:original-link" class="proposition">

<a id="red:link-equations"></a>
**Proposition 6** (Smooth original link). *Every point of $\partial\{u>0\}\setminus\{0\}$ is regular. Put $m_o=d-1$ and $g_o=u|_{S^{m_o}}$. The set $\{g_o>0\}$ is nonempty and proper, has finitely many connected components, and their closures are pairwise disjoint compact smooth manifolds with boundary. On each such component $\Omega_o$, the restriction of $g_o$ is smooth up to the boundary from within $\Omega_o$ and satisfies $$\label{red:link-equations}
 \Delta_{S^{m_o}}g_o=-m_o g_o\quad\hbox{in }\Omega_o,
 \qquad g_o=0,\quad \nabla_{S^{m_o}}g_o=N_o
       \quad\hbox{on }\partial\Omega_o,$$ where $N_o$ is the inward spherical unit normal. In particular, $\partial\Omega_o\ne\varnothing$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-original-link"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-original-link</summary>

*Proof.* At a nonzero free-boundary point $x_0$, choose a blowup $U$ from Proposition <a href="#red:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:regularity">3</a>. By Lemma <a href="#red:translation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:translation">4</a>, in orthogonal coordinates $\mathbb R^d=x_0^\perp\times\mathbb R$ it has the form $U(y,s)=v(y)$. The factor $v$ is locally Lipschitz, nonnegative, nonzero and one-homogeneous. Lemma <a href="#red:factor-minimality" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:factor-minimality">5</a> shows that it is a global minimizer. Since its dimension is $d-1<d_*$, it is flat. Therefore $U$ itself is a half-space solution, and Proposition <a href="#red:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:regularity">3</a> makes $x_0$ regular.

<a id="red:polar-laplacian"></a>
The spherical positive set is nonempty by nontriviality. The polar-coordinate formula for the Euclidean Laplacian of $u(ry)=r g_o(y)$ gives $$\label{red:polar-laplacian}
 \Delta_{\mathbb R^d}u(ry)=r^{-1}
       \bigl(\Delta_{S^{m_o}}g_o(y)+m_o g_o(y)\bigr)$$ in the positive phase. If $g_o>0$ on the entire sphere, integrating the harmonicity identity over the sphere would give $m_o\int g_o=0$, a contradiction. Thus its positive set is proper.

The conical free boundary has the radial direction tangent to it at every nonzero point: the curve $r\mapsto rx$ lies in that free boundary. Consequently its smooth hypersurface is transverse to the unit sphere, and their intersection is a smooth hypersurface of $S^{m_o}$. Its regular charts have the spherical positive phase exactly on one side. Each point of the sphere therefore has a neighborhood whose intersection with $\{g_o>0\}$ is either connected or empty (using a small ball for points away from the free boundary). A finite subcover of the sphere shows that there are only finitely many positive components. If two component closures met, their common point would be a free-boundary point; the connected positive side of a regular chart there would meet both components, forcing them to coincide. The same charts show that each closure is a smooth compact domain. Each component has nonempty boundary because the sphere is connected and the positive set is proper.

Smoothness of $u$ from its positive side gives smoothness of $g_o$ on these closures. Equation <a href="#red:polar-laplacian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:polar-laplacian">[red:polar-laplacian]</a> gives the interior equation in <a href="#red:link-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:link-equations">[red:link-equations]</a>. On the boundary, the radial derivative of $u$ is $g_o=0$, so the Euclidean gradient is tangent to the sphere. Equation <a href="#red:bernoulli" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:bernoulli">[red:bernoulli]</a> then gives the remaining boundary conditions. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-red-nonflat-component"></a>
<div id="red:nonflat-component" class="lemma">

<a id="red:original-hessian"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Selection of a nonflat component). *There is a component $\Omega_o$ in Proposition <a href="#red:original-link" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:original-link">6</a> such that $$\label{red:original-hessian}
 T_o:=\nabla^2_{S^{m_o}}g_o+g_o\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}\not\equiv0
 \quad\hbox{on }\Omega_o.$$ Here $\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$ denotes the spherical metric, viewed as a symmetric two-tensor.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-nonflat-component"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-nonflat-component</summary>

*Proof.* For $y$ in the spherical positive set and $r>0$, $$\nabla_{\mathbb R^d}u(ry)=g_o(y)y+\nabla_{S^{m_o}}g_o(y).$$ Differentiating this formula, or using Euler’s identity for the radial entries, gives <a id="red:ambient-hessian"></a>$$\label{red:ambient-hessian}
 D^2u(ry)[y,\cdot]=0,
 \qquad
 D^2u(ry)[v_1,v_2]=r^{-1}T_o(y)[v_1,v_2]
 \quad(v_1,v_2\perp y).$$ Suppose that $T_o$ vanished identically on every positive component. Then $D^2u=0$ on each corresponding connected open cone $D_i$, so $u(x)=b_i\cdot x+c_i$ there. Homogeneity forces $c_i=0$, and positivity forces $b_i\ne0$. Thus $D_i\subset H_i:=\{b_i\cdot x>0\}$. The set $D_i$ is also closed relative to $H_i$: if $x\in\overline{D_i}\cap H_i$, continuity gives $u(x)=b_i\cdot x>0$; a small positive neighborhood of $x$ meets $D_i$, hence belongs to the same component. Since $H_i$ is connected, $D_i=H_i$. The Bernoulli condition at any nonzero point of its boundary gives $|b_i|=1$.

Two disjoint open half-spaces through the origin must have opposite unit normals: if their normals are $e,f$ with $f\ne-e$, the vector $e+f$ lies in both. But opposite half-spaces have spherical closures meeting along the equator $e^\perp\cap S^{m_o}$, contrary to the pairwise disjoint closures in Proposition <a href="#red:original-link" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:original-link">6</a>. Thus there is exactly one positive component, and $u=(x\cdot e)_+$, contrary to nonflatness. This proves <a href="#red:original-hessian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:original-hessian">[red:original-hessian]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="red:suspension-subsection"></a>
#### Suspension and the working domain

<a id="stmt-red-suspension"></a>
<div id="red:suspension" class="proposition">

<a id="red:suspension-coordinates"></a>
**Proposition 8** (Suspension preserves global minimality). *If $u\geq0$ is a global minimizer in $\mathbb R^k$, then $\widetilde u(x,s)=u(x)$ is a global minimizer in $\mathbb R^{k+1}$. If $u$ is the cone in <a href="#red:first-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:first-dimension">[red:first-dimension]</a> with $d=5$, the singular free-boundary set of $\widetilde u$ is exactly $\{0\}\times\mathbb R$. For the component chosen in Lemma <a href="#red:nonflat-component" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:nonflat-component">7</a>, the corresponding positive component on $S^5$ is $$\label{red:suspension-coordinates}
 \widetilde\Omega
 =\bigl\{\Phi(t,y):=(\sin t\,y,\cos t):
              0<t<\pi,\ y\in\Omega_o\bigr\},
 \qquad
 \widetilde g(\Phi(t,y))=\sin t\,g_o(y).$$ Its boundary consists of the smooth side $\Phi((0,\pi)\times\partial\Omega_o)$ and the two singular tips $p_+=(0,1)$ and $p_-=(0,-1)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-red-suspension"><summary>Proof of stmt-red-suspension</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a ball $\mathcal B=B_R((x_c,s_c))\subset\mathbb R^{k+1}$ and a nonnegative $V\in H^1(\mathcal B)$ with $V-\widetilde u\in H^1_0(\mathcal B)$. Extend that difference by zero to $F\in H^1(\mathbb R^{k+1})$. For $|s-s_c|<R$, let $$B_s=B_{\sqrt{R^2-(s-s_c)^2}}(x_c)$$ be the horizontal slice. For almost every such $s$, $F(\cdot,s)|_{B_s}\in H^1_0(B_s)$. To see the zero-boundary assertion without a trace regularity assumption on $V$, approximate $F$ in $H^1(\mathbb R^{k+1})$ by the zero extensions of functions in $C_c^\infty(\mathcal B)$. After passing to a subsequence, Fubini’s Theorem gives convergence of their slices to $F(\cdot,s)$ in $H^1(\mathbb R^k)$ for almost every $s$. Each approximating slice lies in $C_c^\infty(B_s)$, so the assertion follows by closure.

For almost every $s$, $V_s=V(\cdot,s)$ is therefore an admissible nonnegative competitor for $u$ in $B_s$. Integrating its minimizing inequality and using the nonnegativity of the additional derivative energy, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
 J(\widetilde u;\mathcal B)
 &=\int_{s_c-R}^{s_c+R}J(u;B_s)\,ds\\
 &\leq\int_{s_c-R}^{s_c+R}J(V_s;B_s)\,ds
 \leq J(V;\mathcal B).
\end{aligned}$$ This proves the full ballwise minimizing property, for arbitrary centers and arbitrary admissible Sobolev competitors. One-homogeneity and nontriviality are plainly preserved. Flatness of $\widetilde u$ would force its half-space normal to have zero last coordinate, and would therefore force $u$ to be flat.

In the conical case, $u(0)=0$ and a positive ray approaches the origin, so $0\in\partial\{u>0\}$. The product identity $$\partial\{\widetilde u>0\}=\partial\{u>0\}\times\mathbb R$$ shows that all its off-axis points are regular: their positive phase is the product of a smooth one-sided regular chart for $u$ and an interval. At any axis point $z_0=(0,s_0)$, every rescaling is exactly $$\frac{\widetilde u(z_0+r(x,s))}{r}
 =\frac{u(rx)}r=\widetilde u(x,s).$$ This is nonflat, so no axis point is regular. The axis meets $S^5$ only in $p_+$ and $p_-$.

Away from these two points, $\Phi$ is a smooth product coordinate map from $(0,\pi)\times S^4$ to $S^5\setminus\{p_+,p_-\}$. Positivity in these coordinates is precisely $g_o(y)>0$, so connected components are as in <a href="#red:suspension-coordinates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="red:suspension-coordinates">[red:suspension-coordinates]</a>. Since $\overline{\Omega_o}$ is compact, its suspended closure is obtained by adding the two endpoints of the interval, with each endpoint collapsed to its tip. Its remaining boundary is exactly the stated smooth side. ◻

</details>

<a id="red:working-domain"></a>
###### Notation for the remaining argument.

Fix the original component $\Omega_o\subset S^{m_o}$ selected in Lemma <a href="#red:nonflat-component" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:nonflat-component">7</a>, where $m_o\in\{4,5\}$. If $m_o=5$, set $\Omega=\Omega_o$, $g=g_o$, and $\Sigma=\varnothing$. If $m_o=4$, set $\Omega=\widetilde\Omega$, $g=\widetilde g$, and $\Sigma=\{p_+,p_-\}$. In either case $\Omega\subset S^5$ is connected, and $g$ comes from a nonnegative one-homogeneous global minimizer in $\mathbb R^6$. The closure is smooth away from $\Sigma$, with positive phase on one side of its regular boundary $$\partial_{\mathrm{reg}}\Omega:=\partial\Omega\setminus\Sigma.$$ All spherical boundary values below are taken from the positive side. Tests on $\overline\Omega$ will be smooth and supported away from $\Sigma$. Every compact portion of the selected Euclidean cone’s closure that avoids the origin and, in the suspended case, the axis is separated from all other positive components. This follows from the disjoint compact original closures, and in the suspension from the same fact on any closed subinterval of $(0,\pi)$. Consequently the local variations on this component can be made as variations of the global minimizer. No minimizing property of the selected component extended by zero is needed.

<a id="geo:section"></a>
### Spherical geometry and the stability inequality

<span id="sec:geometry" label="sec:geometry"></span>

Let $\Omega_o\subset S^{m_o}$, $m_o\in\{4,5\}$, be the original component supplied by Proposition <a href="#red:original-link" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:original-link">6</a> and Lemma <a href="#red:nonflat-component" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:nonflat-component">7</a>. Its closure is a compact smooth manifold with nonempty boundary. The function $g_o$ is smooth up to that boundary, is positive in $\Omega_o$, and satisfies $$\Delta g_o=-m_o g_o,\qquad
 g_o=0,\quad \nabla g_o=N_o\quad\hbox{on }\partial\Omega_o,$$ where $N_o$ is the inward unit normal. Moreover, $\nabla^2g_o+g_o\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$ does not vanish identically. We give the identities in a general tangent dimension $m$. The compact original component first provides strict gradient and boundary-sign bounds; we then transport these bounds to the working link and derive its stability inequality. All spherical derivatives below are covariant, and $\Delta$ on tensors is the rough Laplacian $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\nabla^2$. Repeated indices in an orthonormal frame are summed.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The spherical Hessian and its boundary values

<a id="geo:ambient-hessian"></a>
For a smooth solution of $\Delta g=-mg$ on a domain in $S^m$, set <a id="geo:data"></a>$$\label{geo:data}
 p=\nabla g,\qquad T=\nabla^2g+g\mathop{\mathrm{Id}},\qquad
 B_{ijk}=\nabla_iT_{jk},\qquad w=|T|,\qquad s^2=g^2+|p|^2.$$ The notation $s^2$ denotes the displayed smooth function, including at points where it vanishes. If $u(ry)=rg(y)$ is the homogeneous extension, then in the radial and tangential orthonormal frame $$\label{geo:ambient-hessian}
 D^2u(ry)=r^{-1}
 \begin{pmatrix}0&0\\0&T(y)\end{pmatrix}.$$ Indeed, Euler’s identity gives $D^2u\,y=0$, and differentiation of $\nabla_{\mathbb R^{m+1}}u=g y+p$ along a tangent vector gives the tangential block $\nabla^2g+g\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$ at radius one. Homogeneity then gives the factor $r^{-1}$. The same gradient formula identifies $s^2$ with $|\nabla_{\mathbb R^{m+1}}u|^2$ on the unit sphere.

<a id="stmt-geo-interior-identities"></a>
<div id="geo:interior-identities" class="proposition">

<a id="geo:commutator"></a>
<a id="geo:bochner"></a>
<a id="geo:first-jets"></a>
<a id="geo:s-identities"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Interior identities). *The tensor $T$ is symmetric and trace-free, and $B$ is fully symmetric and trace-free in every pair of slots. In particular $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}T=0$. The following identities hold: $$\begin{aligned}
 \nabla_lB_{ijk}-\nabla_iB_{ljk}
 &=\delta_{lj}T_{ik}+\delta_{lk}T_{ij}
   -\delta_{ij}T_{lk}-\delta_{ik}T_{lj},
 \label{geo:commutator}\\
 \Delta T&=mT,
 &\Delta w^2&=2m w^2+2|B|^2,
 \label{geo:bochner}\\
 \nabla_l p_i&=T_{li}-g\delta_{li},
 &\nabla_l g&=p_l,
 \label{geo:first-jets}\\
 \nabla s^2&=2Tp,
 &\Delta s^2&=2w^2.
 \label{geo:s-identities}
\end{aligned}$$ On the open set $\{w>0\}$ one also has <a id="geo:w-gradient"></a>$$\label{geo:w-gradient}
 \nabla_l w=w^{-1}T_{jk}B_{ljk},\qquad |\nabla w|\le |B|.$$ The inequality $|\nabla(w^2)|^2\le4w^2|B|^2$ holds everywhere, including at $w=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-interior-identities"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-interior-identities</summary>

*Proof.* The trace equation is $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}T=\Delta g+mg=0$. On the unit sphere the commutation rule for the covector $p$ gives $$\nabla_i(\nabla^2g)_{jk}-\nabla_j(\nabla^2g)_{ik}
 =\delta_{ik}p_j-\delta_{jk}p_i.$$ Adding the derivatives of $g\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$ cancels the right side. Thus $T$ is Codazzi: $\nabla_iT_{jk}=\nabla_jT_{ik}$. Together with symmetry in $j,k$, this proves full symmetry of $B$. Differentiating $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}T=0$ shows $B_{ijj}=0$; full symmetry gives all other traces and hence $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}T=0$.

For clarity, the same curvature convention on a covariant two-tensor is $$([\nabla_l,\nabla_i]T)_{jk}
 =\delta_{lj}T_{ik}+\delta_{lk}T_{ij}
  -\delta_{ij}T_{lk}-\delta_{ik}T_{lj}.$$ This proves <a href="#geo:commutator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:commutator">[geo:commutator]</a>. Substitute $(l,i,j,k)=(i,j,i,k)$ there and sum over $i$. By symmetry and the zero traces of $B$, the left side is $\Delta T_{jk}$, whereas the right side is $$mT_{jk}+T_{jk}-T_{jk}-\delta_{jk}\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}T=mT_{jk}.$$ The product rule for $|T|^2$ now proves <a href="#geo:bochner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:bochner">[geo:bochner]</a>. The definitions give <a href="#geo:first-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:first-jets">[geo:first-jets]</a> and $$\nabla_l s^2=2gp_l+2p_i(T_{li}-g\delta_{li})=2T_{li}p_i.$$ Taking the divergence and using $\mathop{\mathrm{div}}T=0$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}T=0$ gives $\Delta s^2=2T_{li}(T_{li}-g\delta_{li})=2w^2$. Finally, differentiating $w^2$ and applying Cauchy–Schwarz proves both gradient estimates. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-geo-gradient-bound"></a>
<div id="geo:gradient-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10** (Strict gradient bound on the original component). *On $\Omega_o$ one has $s_o^2<1$. At every boundary point, put <a id="geo:H-definition"></a>$$\label{geo:H-definition}
 H_o=-T_o(N_o,N_o).$$ Then <a id="geo:strict-boundary-sign"></a>$$\label{geo:strict-boundary-sign}
 H_o>0,\qquad T_op_o=-H_op_o,\qquad w_o>0
 \quad\hbox{on }\partial\Omega_o.$$ Consequently $w_o$ has a positive minimum on $\partial\Omega_o$, and its zero set is a compact subset of $\Omega_o$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-gradient-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-gradient-bound</summary>

*Proof.* The boundary conditions give $s_o^2=1$ on $\partial\Omega_o$. By <a href="#geo:s-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:s-identities">[geo:s-identities]</a>, $s_o^2$ is subharmonic. The maximum principle on the compact closure gives $s_o^2\le1$. If equality held at an interior point, the strong maximum principle would make $s_o^2$ constant on the connected component, and then $\Delta s_o^2=2w_o^2$ would imply $T_o\equiv0$. Thus $s_o^2<1$ inside.

The smooth boundary has the interior sphere property at each point. The Hopf boundary lemma therefore gives $\partial_{N_o}s_o^2<0$. Since $p_o=N_o$ there, <a href="#geo:s-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:s-identities">[geo:s-identities]</a> identifies this derivative as $2T_o(N_o,N_o)$, proving $H_o>0$. For a tangent vector $\tau$ to $\partial\Omega_o$, tangential differentiation of $s_o^2=1$ similarly gives $T_o(\tau,N_o)=0$. This proves the eigenvector assertion and $w_o\ge H_o>0$. Continuity and compactness of the boundary give a positive minimum of $w_o$ and a neighborhood of the boundary disjoint from its zero set. ◻

</details>

The remaining boundary formulas are local. Thus they apply on the original boundary and, once the strict sign has been transported, on the regular side of the suspension.

<a id="stmt-geo-boundary-jets"></a>
<div id="geo:boundary-jets" class="proposition">

<a id="geo:even-boundary-jets"></a>
<a id="geo:odd-boundary-jets"></a>
**Proposition 11** (Boundary jets). *Suppose $g=0$, $p=N$, and $T(N,N)=-H<0$ on a smooth boundary portion, where $N$ is inward. Let Greek indices be tangent to this boundary and put $$\mathfrak h_{\alpha\beta}
 =\langle\nabla_{\alpha}N,e_\beta\rangle.$$ Then $T_{N\alpha}=0$, $T_{\alpha\beta}=\mathfrak h_{\alpha\beta}$, and $H=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\mathfrak h$; in particular $H$ is a sum of principal curvatures with the specified convention, without division by $m-1$. If $\nabla^{\partial}$ is the boundary connection, all components of $B$ are described, up to permutation of slots, by $$\begin{aligned}
 B_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
   &=(\nabla^{\partial}_{\alpha}\mathfrak h)_{\beta\gamma},
 &B_{NN\alpha}&=-\partial_\alpha H,
 \label{geo:even-boundary-jets}\\
 B_{N\alpha\beta}
   &=T_{NN}T_{\alpha\beta}-(T^2)_{\alpha\beta},
 &B_{NNN}&=w^2.
 \label{geo:odd-boundary-jets}
\end{aligned}$$ The components with an odd number of normal slots are thus determined by the pointwise tensor $T$; the other components involve tangential derivatives of curvature. The tensor $V$ below encodes this odd-normal part after normalization. Here $w>0$. Define the normalized tensors and eigenvalue $$A=T/w,\qquad \lambda=T_{NN}/w=-H/w,
 \qquad S_0=\lambda A-A^2,$$ and the symmetric tensor <a id="geo:boundary-V"></a>$$\label{geo:boundary-V}
 V_{ijk}=p_i(S_0)_{jk}+p_j(S_0)_{ki}+p_k(S_0)_{ij}
           +p_ip_jp_k.$$ Then $|p|=1$, $Ap=\lambda p$, $\lambda<0$, $S_0p=0$, $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}S_0=-1$, and $V$ is trace-free. The tensors $B/w^2$ and $V$ agree in every component with an odd number of normal slots. Moreover, <a id="geo:normal-w"></a>$$\label{geo:normal-w}
 V_{Njk}=(S_0+p\otimes p)_{jk},\qquad
 \partial_N w=w^2\bigl(2\lambda-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^3\bigr).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-boundary-jets"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-boundary-jets</summary>

*Proof.* On the boundary, tangential differentiation of $|p|^2=1$ gives $T_{N\alpha}=0$, and $\nabla_\alpha p=T e_\alpha$ because $g=0$. Since $p=N$ along the boundary, this proves $T_{\alpha\beta}=\mathfrak h_{\alpha\beta}$. The trace equation for $T$ gives $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}\mathfrak h=-T_{NN}=H$. In differentiating three tangential entries of $T$, the normal connection terms pair with $T(N,e_\alpha)=0$. This yields the first formula in <a href="#geo:even-boundary-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:even-boundary-jets">[geo:even-boundary-jets]</a>. Likewise, $$\partial_\alpha T(N,N)
 =B_{\alpha NN}+2T(\nabla_\alpha N,N)=B_{\alpha NN},$$ which proves the second formula by symmetry of $B$.

For a tangent field $e_\alpha$, differentiate $T(N,e_\alpha)=0$ along $e_\beta$. Since $\langle N,\nabla_\beta e_\alpha\rangle
=-\mathfrak h_{\beta\alpha}$, this gives $$0=B_{\beta N\alpha}+(T^2)_{\beta\alpha}
     -T_{NN}T_{\beta\alpha}.$$ This proves the first formula in <a href="#geo:odd-boundary-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:odd-boundary-jets">[geo:odd-boundary-jets]</a>. Taking its tangential trace gives $$\sum_\alpha B_{N\alpha\alpha}
 =T_{NN}\sum_\alpha T_{\alpha\alpha}
    -\sum_{\alpha,\beta}T_{\alpha\beta}^2
 =-T_{NN}^2-\sum_{\alpha,\beta}T_{\alpha\beta}^2=-w^2.$$ The zero trace of $B$ then gives $B_{NNN}=w^2$.

Normalization gives $S_0p=0$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}S_0=-|A|^2=-1$. Consequently $V_{ijj}=p_i\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}S_0+2(S_0p)_i+p_i=0$. In an orthonormal frame with first vector $p=N$, one has $V_{N\alpha\beta}=(S_0)_{\alpha\beta}$ and $V_{NNN}=1$. These are exactly the normalized identities <a href="#geo:odd-boundary-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:odd-boundary-jets">[geo:odd-boundary-jets]</a>, and $V_{Njk}=S_{0,jk}+p_jp_k$. Finally, $T$ has only its normal-normal and tangential-tangential blocks, so the odd-normal formulas account for the entire contraction $T_{jk}B_{Njk}$. Thus $$\partial_Nw=w^{-1}T_{jk}B_{Njk}
 =w^2 A_{jk}(S_0+p\otimes p)_{jk}
 =w^2(2\lambda-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^3),$$ as asserted. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Transport to a suspension

<a id="stmt-geo-suspension-data"></a>
<div id="geo:suspension-data" class="proposition">

<a id="geo:suspended-first-jets"></a>
<a id="geo:suspended-T"></a>
<a id="geo:suspended-B"></a>
<a id="geo:suspended-norms"></a>
<a id="geo:suspended-measures"></a>
**Proposition 12** (Suspended data). *If $m_o=4$, write $\rho=\sin t$, $c=\cos t$ and parameterize the regular part of the suspended component by $$(t,y)\longmapsto (\rho y,c),\qquad
 0<t<\pi,\quad y\in\Omega_o.$$ Its metric is $dt^2+\rho^2 ds_{S^4}^2$, and $\widetilde g=\rho g_o$. With $E_0=\partial_t$ and $E_\alpha=\rho^{-1}e_\alpha$ for a lifted orthonormal frame on $S^4$, the data satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
 \widetilde p_0&=c g_o,
 &\widetilde p_\alpha&=(p_o)_\alpha,
 &\widetilde s^2&=s_o^2,
 \label{geo:suspended-first-jets}\\
 \widetilde T_{00}&=0,
 &\widetilde T_{0\alpha}&=0,
 &\widetilde T_{\alpha\beta}&=\rho^{-1}(T_o)_{\alpha\beta}.
 \label{geo:suspended-T}
\end{aligned}$$ The only possibly nonzero components of $\widetilde B$ are $$\label{geo:suspended-B}
 \widetilde B_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
   =\rho^{-2}(B_o)_{\alpha\beta\gamma},\qquad
 \widetilde B_{0\alpha\beta}
 =\widetilde B_{\alpha0\beta}
 =\widetilde B_{\alpha\beta0}
   =-c\rho^{-2}(T_o)_{\alpha\beta}.$$ In particular, $$\label{geo:suspended-norms}
 \widetilde w=\rho^{-1}w_o,\qquad
 |\widetilde B|^2
 =\rho^{-4}\bigl(|B_o|^2+3c^2w_o^2\bigr),\qquad
 \widetilde H=\rho^{-1}H_o
 \quad\hbox{on the regular side.}$$ The volume and side-boundary measures are $$\label{geo:suspended-measures}
 dV=\rho^4\,dt\,dV_o,\qquad
 d\sigma=\rho^3\,dt\,d\sigma_o.$$ Every local identity of Proposition <a href="#geo:interior-identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:interior-identities">9</a> holds with $m=5$, and Proposition <a href="#geo:boundary-jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:boundary-jets">11</a> holds on the regular side. Also $\widetilde s^2<1$ inside and $\widetilde H,\widetilde w>0$ on that side.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-suspension-data"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-suspension-data</summary>

*Proof.* At a point where the base frame is normal, the warped metric has connection $$\nabla_{E_0}E_0=\nabla_{E_0}E_\alpha=0,\qquad
 \nabla_{E_\alpha}E_0=(c/\rho)E_\alpha,\qquad
 \nabla_{E_\alpha}E_\beta=-(c/\rho)\delta_{\alpha\beta}E_0.$$ The horizontal part of the last expression at other points is the base connection with factor $\rho^{-1}$. Differentiating $\widetilde g=\rho g_o$ gives <a href="#geo:suspended-first-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:suspended-first-jets">[geo:suspended-first-jets]</a>. Its Hessian has entries $$(\nabla^2\widetilde g)_{00}=-\rho g_o,
 \quad (\nabla^2\widetilde g)_{0\alpha}=0,
 \quad (\nabla^2\widetilde g)_{\alpha\beta}
 =\rho^{-1}(\nabla_o^2 g_o)_{\alpha\beta}
   +(c^2/\rho)g_o\delta_{\alpha\beta}.$$ Adding $\widetilde g\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$ proves <a href="#geo:suspended-T" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:suspended-T">[geo:suspended-T]</a>. Differentiating that tensor with the displayed connection gives <a href="#geo:suspended-B" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:suspended-B">[geo:suspended-B]</a>; entries with two or three zero slots vanish. Each term with exactly one zero slot occurs in three positions, which proves the factor $3$ in <a href="#geo:suspended-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:suspended-norms">[geo:suspended-norms]</a>.

The inward normal to the side is the horizontal lift of $N_o$. Thus the normal-normal entry of <a href="#geo:suspended-T" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:suspended-T">[geo:suspended-T]</a> gives $\widetilde H=\rho^{-1}H_o$. The metric on the side is $dt^2+\rho^2 ds_{\partial\Omega_o}^2$, proving <a href="#geo:suspended-measures" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:suspended-measures">[geo:suspended-measures]</a>. The trace of <a href="#geo:suspended-T" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:suspended-T">[geo:suspended-T]</a> is zero, so $\Delta\widetilde g=-5\widetilde g$. The interior identities follow locally from Proposition <a href="#geo:interior-identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:interior-identities">9</a>. The bounds for $s^2$, $H$ and $w$ follow from the original compact component and <a href="#geo:suspended-first-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:suspended-first-jets">[geo:suspended-first-jets]</a>–<a href="#geo:suspended-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:suspended-norms">[geo:suspended-norms]</a>; no maximum principle at a tip is used. The local boundary hypotheses and hence Proposition <a href="#geo:boundary-jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:boundary-jets">11</a> hold on the regular side. ◻

</details>

We now use the unadorned notation $\Omega,g,p,T,B,w,H$ for the working component in $S^5$ defined at the end of Section <a href="#sec:reductions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reductions">[sec:reductions]</a>. It is either $\Omega_o$ when $m_o=5$, or the above suspension when $m_o=4$. All boundary integrals mean integrals over the regular boundary, with the two tips omitted in the suspended case. The original data retain the subscript $o$ when needed.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Second variation under compact perturbations

We derive stability directly from the minimizing property, including its sign and test class. The argument works in either original ambient dimension and in the six-dimensional suspension.

<a id="stmt-geo-euclidean-stability"></a>
<div id="geo:euclidean-stability" class="proposition">

<a id="geo:euclidean-inequality"></a>
**Proposition 13** (Euclidean stability on a component). *Let $D$ be the selected positivity component of a global minimizing cone in $\mathbb R^{m+1}$. Let $\phi$ be any real smooth function up to the regular boundary of $D$, with compact support in that regular closure away from the vertex and from any other singular points. Then $$\label{geo:euclidean-inequality}
 \int_D |\nabla\phi|^2\,dx
 \ge \int_{\partial D}H_E\phi^2\,d\sigma_E,
 \qquad H_E=-D^2u(N_E,N_E),$$ where $N_E$ is the inward Euclidean unit normal. No restriction is imposed on the boundary trace or normal derivative of $\phi$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-euclidean-stability"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-euclidean-stability</summary>

*Proof.* Every regular free-boundary point has a neighborhood where the positive phase is the single side belonging to $D$. Compactness of the boundary portion meeting $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\phi$ gives finitely many such neighborhoods and a collar separated from every other positivity component and every singular point. Choose the collar over a slightly larger boundary portion, so that the extension of $\phi$ can vanish near its lateral edge. Extend $\phi$ smoothly across this boundary with compact support in these neighborhoods. Also extend the positive side of $u$ smoothly to a function $F$ on the collar, with its interior jets unchanged. Since $u=0$ and $\nabla u=N_E$ on the boundary, shrinking the collar makes $F$ negative on its exterior side and positive on its interior side. Such an extension need only be smooth to finite order sufficient for the expansions below; smooth boundary regularity supplies this, and a partition of unity joins the local extensions while preserving the interior function and its jets.

For a real parameter $\varepsilon$ of either sufficiently small sign, use $(F+\varepsilon\phi)_+$ in the collar, $u+\varepsilon\phi$ on the rest of the affected portion of $D$, and $u$ elsewhere. These definitions agree: $F=u$ on the interior of the collar, and on the compact interior support outside a smaller collar $u$ has a positive minimum. Near the exterior and lateral edges of the modification the function agrees with $u$. The resulting competitor $u_\varepsilon$ is nonnegative, belongs to $H^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$, and has compactly supported difference from $u$ in a containing ball. It is therefore admissible for the original global minimizer. This construction uses no separate minimizing assertion for $D$.

<a id="geo:fixed-domain-variation"></a>
We compute the change in energy in such a ball. First integrate the perturbed density over the fixed original domain $D$, and then account for the signed boundary layer where its positivity set changes. The fixed-domain contribution is $$\begin{aligned}
 &2\varepsilon\int_D\langle\nabla u,\nabla\phi\rangle\,dx
       +\varepsilon^2\int_D|\nabla\phi|^2\,dx\notag\\
 &\hspace{15mm}=-2\varepsilon\int_{\partial D}\phi\,d\sigma_E
       +\varepsilon^2\int_D|\nabla\phi|^2\,dx.
 \label{geo:fixed-domain-variation}
\end{aligned}$$ Here harmonicity eliminates the interior term and the outward normal is $-N_E$, so $\partial_{-N_E}u=-1$. All integrations by parts have compact support on the regular portion.

Use coordinates $(y,\xi)$ obtained by flowing from the original boundary under $$V_F=\frac{\nabla F}{|\nabla F|^2},\qquad \xi=F.$$ The denominator is nonzero on a sufficiently small compact collar. At $\xi=0$, $V_F=N_E$. If $j(y,\xi)$ is the volume Jacobian relative to $d\sigma_E(y)\,d\xi$, then <a id="geo:flow-jacobian"></a>$$\label{geo:flow-jacobian}
 j(y,0)=1,\qquad
 \partial_\xi j(y,0)=\mathop{\mathrm{div}}V_F(y,0)
 =\Delta F-2F_{N_EN_E}=2H_E.$$ The equality $\Delta F=0$ on the original boundary follows from matching the interior second derivatives; harmonicity outside $D$ is unnecessary. Moreover, $$\left.\partial_\xi|\nabla F|^2\right|_{\xi=0}
 =2F_{N_EN_E}=-2H_E,$$ and hence <a id="geo:edge-density-derivative"></a>$$\label{geo:edge-density-derivative}
 \left.\partial_\xi\bigl[(1+|\nabla F|^2)j\bigr]
 \right|_{\xi=0}=2H_E.$$

Write $\phi_0=\phi(y,0)$ and $\phi_N=\partial_{N_E}\phi(y,0)$. The new zero surface has coordinate $b_\varepsilon(y)$ determined by $b_\varepsilon+\varepsilon\phi(y,b_\varepsilon)=0$. The implicit function theorem and $\partial_\xi\phi(y,0)=\phi_N$ give, uniformly on the compact patch, <a id="geo:moving-edge"></a>$$\label{geo:moving-edge}
 b_\varepsilon=-\varepsilon\phi_0
      +\varepsilon^2\phi_0\phi_N+O(|\varepsilon|^3).$$ Throughout the layer $|\xi|\le C|\varepsilon|$, its perturbed energy density is <a id="geo:layer-density"></a>$$\label{geo:layer-density}
 (1+|\nabla(F+\varepsilon\phi)|^2)j
 =2+2\varepsilon\phi_N+2H_E\xi
      +O(\varepsilon^2+|\varepsilon\xi|+\xi^2).$$ Integrating this density from $b_\varepsilon$ to $0$ is the signed correction, whether the phase expands or contracts. The terms of order at most two are <a id="geo:layer-cancellation"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{b_\varepsilon}^{0}
       (1+|\nabla(F+\varepsilon\phi)|^2)j\,d\xi
 &=-2b_\varepsilon-2\varepsilon\phi_N b_\varepsilon
      -H_E b_\varepsilon^2+O(|\varepsilon|^3)\notag\\
 &=2\varepsilon\phi_0
   +\varepsilon^2\bigl(-2\phi_0\phi_N
          +2\phi_0\phi_N-H_E\phi_0^2\bigr)
       +O(|\varepsilon|^3)\notag\\
 &=2\varepsilon\phi_0-\varepsilon^2H_E\phi_0^2
       +O(|\varepsilon|^3).
 \label{geo:layer-cancellation}
\end{aligned}$$ This displays the cancellation of the normal-derivative terms. The uniform remainder is integrable over the compact affected boundary. Adding <a href="#geo:fixed-domain-variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:fixed-domain-variation">[geo:fixed-domain-variation]</a> also cancels the linear terms, leaving $$J(u_\varepsilon)-J(u)
 =\varepsilon^2\left(
      \int_D|\nabla\phi|^2\,dx
        -\int_{\partial D}H_E\phi^2\,d\sigma_E\right)
       +o(\varepsilon^2).$$ The energies here are in a ball containing the modification. Minimality and division by $\varepsilon^2$ prove <a href="#geo:euclidean-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:euclidean-inequality">[geo:euclidean-inequality]</a>. ◻

</details>

The angular constant comes from the sharp radial Hardy threshold, which also enters the cone instability criteria in <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[JerisonSavin2015](#ref-JerisonSavin2015), Propositions 2.1–2.2]. We include the radial calculation to specify the compact supports and the order of limits.

<a id="stmt-geo-spherical-stability"></a>
<div id="geo:spherical-stability" class="corollary">

<a id="geo:spherical-inequality"></a>
**Corollary 14** (Spherical stability). *For every real function $v$ smooth up to the regular boundary of the working component $\Omega\subset S^5$, with compact support in its closure away from the tips if present, $$\label{geo:spherical-inequality}
 \int_{\partial\Omega}H v^2\,d\sigma
 \le \int_\Omega\bigl(|\nabla v|^2+4v^2\bigr)\,dV.$$ The trace of $v$ on the regular boundary is arbitrary.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-geo-spherical-stability"><summary>Proof of stmt-geo-spherical-stability</summary>

*Proof.* More generally, consider a link component $\Omega\subset S^m$ with angular tests supported in its regular closure, and write $D=\{ry:r>0,\ y\in\Omega\}$. On its regular side the normal is tangent to $S^m$, and <a href="#geo:ambient-hessian" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:ambient-hessian">[geo:ambient-hessian]</a> gives $H_E(ry)=r^{-1}H(y)$. Polar volume has factor $r^m\,dr$ and side area has factor $r^{m-1}\,dr$. For $\zeta\in C_c^\infty((0,\infty))$ and an allowed angular test $v$, put $\phi(ry)=\zeta(r)v(y)$ in Proposition <a href="#geo:euclidean-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:euclidean-stability">13</a>. It gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \left(\int_0^\infty r^{m-2}\zeta^2\,dr\right)
       \int_{\partial\Omega}Hv^2\,d\sigma
 &\le
 \left(\int_0^\infty r^{m-2}\zeta^2\,dr\right)
       \int_\Omega|\nabla v|^2\,dV\\
 &\quad+
 \left(\int_0^\infty r^m|\zeta'|^2\,dr\right)
       \int_\Omega v^2\,dV.
\end{aligned}$$ For each fixed $\zeta$ and $v$, the Euclidean support lies in a finite annulus and is separated from the singular set and other components, so the preceding second variation applies.

<a id="geo:hardy-limit"></a>
Set $\beta=(m-1)/2$. For $f_\zeta(r)=r^\beta\zeta(r)$, integration of the cross term gives the radial Hardy identity $$\int_0^\infty r^m|\zeta'|^2\,dr
  -\beta^2\int_0^\infty r^{m-2}\zeta^2\,dr
 =\int_0^\infty r|f_\zeta'(r)|^2\,dr\ge0.$$ To approach its constant, choose nonzero $\psi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$ and set $\zeta_k(r)=r^{-\beta}\psi((\log r)/k)$. The substitution $\tau=(\log r)/k$ yields $$\label{geo:hardy-limit}
 \frac{\int_0^\infty r^m|\zeta_k'|^2\,dr}
      {\int_0^\infty r^{m-2}\zeta_k^2\,dr}
 =\beta^2+\frac1{k^2}
          \frac{\int_\mathbb R|\psi'|^2\,d\tau}
               {\int_\mathbb R\psi^2\,d\tau}.$$ Indeed the numerator before division is $k\int_\mathbb R(-\beta\psi+k^{-1}\psi')^2\,d\tau$, and $\int\psi\psi'=0$ by compact support. Divide the separated stability inequality by its positive radial factor and let $k\to\infty$. In tangent dimension $m=5$, one has $\beta^2=4$, proving <a href="#geo:spherical-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:spherical-inequality">[geo:spherical-inequality]</a>. The same computation on an original link with $m_o=4$ would give $9/4$; the working link always has $m=5$.

The order of limits is fixed: for each angular test and each finite $k$, first take $\varepsilon\to0$ in the second variation, and then take $k\to\infty$. Angular cutoffs used later are removed only after <a href="#geo:spherical-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:spherical-inequality">[geo:spherical-inequality]</a> has been obtained for each fixed smooth test. The collar and the allowable size of $\varepsilon$ may depend on these fixed supports. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:certificate"></a>
### A universal algebraic certificate

<span id="cert:section" label="cert:section"></span>

The comparison needed for the stability inequality is a statement about finite-dimensional tensors. We state it with free algebraic data before deriving its geometric consequences. All tensor spaces here are over $\mathbb R^5$, with the Euclidean inner product and full tensor norm; repeated indices range from $1$ to $5$ and are summed. A symmetric tensor is called trace-free if contraction of any two slots is zero. We write $\mathop{\mathrm{STF}}^r(\mathbb R^5)$ for the space of symmetric trace-free tensors of rank $r$. Fix <a id="cert:constants"></a>$$\label{cert:constants}
 a=\frac{29}{50},\qquad Q=250000,\qquad
 \epsilon_* =\frac{438907}{12000000},\qquad
 \delta_* =\frac{6984137359}{31250000000}.$$

<a id="cert:D"></a>
Let $A$ be a symmetric trace-free matrix with $|A|^2=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^2=1$, and let $X$ be a symmetric trace-free three-tensor. Set $$q_l=X_{lij}A_{ij},\qquad \chi=1-|p|^2-g^2,$$ where $p,\ell\in\mathbb R^5$ and $g,w,z\in\mathbb R$ are otherwise free. In particular, $w$ and $z$ need not be positive. On tensor polynomials in $A,p,g,\delta$, define $D_l$ by the product rule and $$\label{cert:D}
 D_l A_{ij}=X_{lij}-q_lA_{ij},\qquad
 D_l p_i=wA_{li}-gz\delta_{li},\qquad
 D_l g=zp_l,\qquad D_l\delta_{ij}=0.$$ This is a first derivative with values in the enlarged polynomial algebra containing $X,w,z$; no derivative of these new variables is used.

<a id="stmt-cert-universal"></a>
<div id="cert:universal" class="proposition">

<a id="cert:interior"></a>
**Proposition 15** (Universal inequalities). *There are rational tensor polynomials $f,U_{jk},K_{lijk},M_l,P_l$ in $A,p,g,\delta$, with the following properties. They are equivariant under orthogonal changes of coordinates, with $f$ invariant. Their coefficients are specified by Table I in Appendix <a href="#app:tables" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:tables">9</a>, using the decoding in Section <a href="#cert:decode" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:decode">6.1</a>. Put $$C_{lijk}=K_{lijk}-K_{iljk},\qquad
 G_{lijk}=\delta_{li}U_{jk}+C_{lijk}.$$ For every choice of the data above with $\chi\ge0$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal I:={}&z^2\left[5U_{jk}A_{jk}
       +C_{lijk}(\delta_{lj}A_{ik}+\delta_{lk}A_{ij})-4f\right]\notag\\
 &+\left[D_lG_{lijk}+(2a-1)q_lG_{lijk}\right]X_{ijk}\notag\\
 &+w\left[D_lM_l+(2a+1)q_lM_l\right]
       +z\left[D_lP_l+2aq_lP_l\right]\notag\\
 &+f|\ell|^2-(D_lf+2aq_lf)\ell_l
 \ \ge\ \epsilon_*(z^2+|\ell|^2).\label{cert:interior}
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, $f\ge\epsilon_*$ whenever $g^2+|p|^2\le1$.*

<a id="cert:boundary"></a>
*For every symmetric trace-free unit matrix $A$ and vector $p$ satisfying $$g=0,\qquad |p|=1,\qquad Ap=\lambda p,\qquad\lambda<0,$$ define $$S_0=\lambda A-A^2,\qquad
 V_{ijk}=p_i(S_0)_{jk}+p_j(S_0)_{ki}+p_k(S_0)_{ij}+p_ip_jp_k.$$ Then $$\label{cert:boundary}
 \mathcal S:=-\lambda f+U_{jk}(S_0+p\otimes p)_{jk}
                 +C_{lijk}p_lV_{ijk}+p_lM_l
 \ \ge\ \delta_*.$$ Moreover, $U$ and $K$ are even in $p$, and $P=0$ when $g=0$.*

</div>

We prove Proposition <a href="#cert:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:universal">15</a> in Section <a href="#sec:certificate-proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificate-proof">6</a>. First, Section <a href="#sec:flux" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:flux">[sec:flux]</a> derives its geometric consequences using only the stated properties. The calculation explains the Laplacian and skew contractions in $G$, the three coefficients of $q$, and the free vector $\ell$.

<a id="flux:section"></a>
### From the algebraic certificate to a geometric flux

<span id="sec:flux" label="sec:flux"></span>

<a id="flux:CG"></a>
We work on the regular part of the spherical component $\Omega\subset S^5$ constructed in Section <a href="#sec:reductions" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:reductions">[sec:reductions]</a>, and put $$\Omega_+=\{x\in\Omega:w(x)>0\},\qquad a=\frac{29}{50}.$$ All assertions up to the side boundary use derivatives from the positive side. In the suspended case the two tips are excluded throughout Section <a href="#sec:flux" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:flux">[sec:flux]</a>. We apply the universal inequalities of Proposition <a href="#cert:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:universal">15</a>, whose proof follows in Section <a href="#sec:certificate-proof" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificate-proof">6</a>. For its coefficient polynomials $f,U,K,M,P$, with their stated slot orders, set $$\label{flux:CG}
 C_{lijk}=K_{lijk}-K_{iljk},\qquad
 G_{lijk}=\delta_{li}U_{jk}+C_{lijk}.$$ Thus $C_{iljk}=-C_{lijk}$. No other slot symmetry of $U$ or $K$ will be needed. The orthogonal equivariance asserted in Proposition <a href="#cert:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:universal">15</a> ensures that all the fields defined below are independent of the orthonormal frame.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Normalized differentiation and the fourth derivatives

<a id="flux:normalized-data"></a>
On $\Omega_+$ define $$\label{flux:normalized-data}
 A=\frac{T}{w},\qquad X=\frac{B}{w},\qquad
 q_l=X_{ljk}A_{jk}.$$ The identities of Proposition <a href="#geo:interior-identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:interior-identities">9</a> show that $A\in\mathop{\mathrm{STF}}^2(\mathbb R^5)$, $|A|=1$, and $X\in\mathop{\mathrm{STF}}^3(\mathbb R^5)$. Moreover, $g^2+|p|^2\leq1$, so these are admissible data for the interior certificate. The homogenizing variable in that certificate is set to $z=1$ in every geometric application.

<a id="stmt-flux-normalized-differentiation"></a>
<div id="flux:normalized-differentiation" class="lemma">

<a id="flux:first-derivatives"></a>
<a id="flux:fourth-elimination"></a>
**Lemma 16**. *At the data <a href="#flux:normalized-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:normalized-data">[flux:normalized-data]</a>, the derivation $D_l$ of <a href="#cert:D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:D">[cert:D]</a>, evaluated at $z=1$, equals covariant differentiation on every coefficient polynomial in $A,p,g,\delta$. In particular, $$\label{flux:first-derivatives}
 \nabla_lw=wq_l,\qquad
 \nabla_lA_{ij}=X_{lij}-q_lA_{ij},\qquad
 \nabla_lp_i=wA_{li}-g\delta_{li},\qquad
 \nabla_lg=p_l.$$ Furthermore, $$\label{flux:fourth-elimination}
 \frac1wG_{lijk}\nabla_lB_{ijk}
 =5U_{jk}A_{jk}
   +C_{lijk}(\delta_{lj}A_{ik}+\delta_{lk}A_{ij}).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-flux-normalized-differentiation"><summary>Proof of stmt-flux-normalized-differentiation</summary>

*Proof.* Differentiating $w^2=T_{jk}T_{jk}$ gives $\nabla_lw=T_{jk}B_{ljk}/w=wq_l$. The quotient rule then gives the formula for $\nabla A$, and $T=\nabla^2g+g\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$ gives the formula for $\nabla p$. In a covariantly constant orthonormal frame at the point under consideration these are exactly the generator rules for $D_l$. The product rule and compatibility of the connection with contractions prove the assertion for every coefficient polynomial. The rules also preserve the normalization constraints: $$D_l(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A)=0,\qquad D_l(|A|^2)=2q_l-2q_l=0$$ on the admissible data.

Here $D_l$ is applied only to coefficient polynomials in $A,p,g,\delta$. Differentiation of the factors $w$ and $X$, which are not coefficient variables, is performed separately. In particular, no rule for $D_lX$ or $D_lq$ is being assumed.

For the $U$ part of <a href="#flux:fourth-elimination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:fourth-elimination">[flux:fourth-elimination]</a>, $$\frac1w\delta_{li}U_{jk}\nabla_lB_{ijk}
 =\frac1wU_{jk}\Delta T_{jk}=5U_{jk}A_{jk}.$$ For the skew part, the curvature identity in Proposition <a href="#geo:interior-identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:interior-identities">9</a> yields $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac1w C_{lijk}\nabla_lB_{ijk}
 &=\frac1{2w}C_{lijk}
       (\nabla_lB_{ijk}-\nabla_iB_{ljk})\\
 &=\frac12 C_{lijk}
   (\delta_{lj}A_{ik}+\delta_{lk}A_{ij}
       -\delta_{ij}A_{lk}-\delta_{ik}A_{lj})\\
 &=C_{lijk}(\delta_{lj}A_{ik}+\delta_{lk}A_{ij}).
\end{aligned}$$ The last equality follows by interchanging the summed indices $l,i$ in the last two terms and using $C_{iljk}=-C_{lijk}$. Equivalently, $C_{lijk}\nabla_lB_{ijk}$ equals $K_{lijk}(\nabla_lB_{ijk}-\nabla_iB_{ljk})$; thus the factor $1/2$ belongs to the formula with $C$, not to the formula with $K$.

This calculation eliminates every undetermined fourth derivative of $g$. Indeed, if two possible tensors $\nabla_lB_{ijk}$ have the same Laplacian contraction and the same skew difference in $l,i$, their difference has zero contraction with $\delta_{li}U_{jk}$ and with $C_{lijk}$. No constraint on the remaining fourth derivative entries is required. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### The square root and the interior comparison

<a id="flux:f-positive"></a>
Proposition <a href="#cert:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:universal">15</a> gives $$\label{flux:f-positive}
 f(A,p,g)\geq\epsilon_*
   =\frac{438907}{12000000}>0$$ on the entire normalized coefficient domain. The verification in Section <a href="#cert:interior-remainder" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:interior-remainder">6.5</a> obtains this bound using the free algebraic data $X=w=z=0$. Since $f$ is independent of $w$, this permitted algebraic substitution does not impose $w=0$ on the geometric data: the fields below are defined on $\Omega_+$, where $w>0$.

<a id="flux:fields"></a>
Define on $\Omega_+$ $$\label{flux:fields}
 h=w^a\sqrt f,\qquad
 Z_l=w^{2a}\bigl(G_{lijk}X_{ijk}+wM_l+P_l\bigr).$$ These fields are smooth on $\Omega_+$ and extend smoothly to each regular side boundary point, since $w>0$ there by Proposition <a href="#geo:boundary-jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:boundary-jets">11</a>.

<a id="stmt-flux-interior-comparison"></a>
<div id="flux:interior-comparison" class="proposition">

<a id="flux:interior-inequality"></a>
**Proposition 17**. *The scalar $h$ and vector field $Z$ satisfy $$\label{flux:interior-inequality}
 \mathop{\mathrm{div}}Z\geq 4h^2+|\nabla h|^2+\epsilon_*w^{2a}
 \qquad\text{on }\Omega_+.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-flux-interior-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-flux-interior-comparison</summary>

<a id="flux:divergence-identity"></a>
*Proof.* First, $$\nabla_lX_{ijk}=\frac1w\nabla_lB_{ijk}-q_lX_{ijk}.$$ Applying the product rule to each of the three summands of $Z$, and then using <a href="#flux:fourth-elimination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:fourth-elimination">[flux:fourth-elimination]</a>, gives the exact identity $$\begin{aligned}
 w^{-2a}\mathop{\mathrm{div}}Z
 ={}&5U_{jk}A_{jk}
       +C_{lijk}(\delta_{lj}A_{ik}+\delta_{lk}A_{ij})\notag\\
 &+\bigl[D_lG_{lijk}+(2a-1)q_lG_{lijk}\bigr]X_{ijk}\notag\\
 &+w\bigl[D_lM_l+(2a+1)q_lM_l\bigr]
       +D_lP_l+2aq_lP_l.\label{flux:divergence-identity}
\end{aligned}$$ Every $D_l$ in Equation <a href="#flux:divergence-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:divergence-identity">[flux:divergence-identity]</a> is evaluated at $z=1$. The three coefficients of $q$ come respectively from differentiating $w^{2a}X$, $w^{2a+1}$, and $w^{2a}$; in the first expression the quotient $X=B/w$ contributes $-q_lX$. Thus their values are exactly $$2a-1=\frac4{25},\qquad 2a+1=\frac{54}{25},\qquad
 2a=\frac{29}{25}.$$

<a id="flux:square-root-derivative"></a>
Put $$\mathcal F_l=D_lf+2aq_lf,\qquad
 \ell_* =\frac{\mathcal F}{2f}.$$ Differentiating $h$ and completing the square give, respectively, $$\label{flux:square-root-derivative}
 \nabla_lh=\frac{w^a}{2\sqrt f}\mathcal F_l,
 \qquad
 |\nabla h|^2=\frac{w^{2a}}{4f}|\mathcal F|^2,$$ and $$f|\ell|^2-\mathcal F\cdot\ell
 =f|\ell-\ell_*|^2-\frac{|\mathcal F|^2}{4f}.$$ In particular, substituting <a href="#flux:divergence-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:divergence-identity">[flux:divergence-identity]</a> into the interior polynomial $\mathcal I$ of <a href="#cert:interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:interior">[cert:interior]</a> gives the identity, for every $\ell$, <a id="flux:square-completion"></a>$$\label{flux:square-completion}
 \mathcal I\big|_{z=1}
 =w^{-2a}\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{div}}Z-4h^2-|\nabla h|^2\bigr)
      +f|\ell-\ell_*|^2.$$ The division by $f$ is justified uniformly by <a href="#flux:f-positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:f-positive">[flux:f-positive]</a>. Evaluating <a href="#cert:interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:interior">[cert:interior]</a> at $\ell=\ell_*$ therefore gives the slightly stronger estimate $$\mathop{\mathrm{div}}Z-4h^2-|\nabla h|^2
 \geq\epsilon_*w^{2a}(1+|\ell_*|^2),$$ which implies <a href="#flux:interior-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:interior-inequality">[flux:interior-inequality]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Boundary reflection and the signed flux

At a regular side boundary point, Proposition <a href="#geo:boundary-jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:boundary-jets">11</a> gives $$g=0,\quad p=N,\quad |p|=1,\quad Ap=\lambda p,
 \qquad \lambda=-\frac H w<0.$$ As in the boundary certificate, define <a id="flux:boundary-model"></a>$$\label{flux:boundary-model}
 S_0=\lambda A-A^2,\qquad
 V_{ijk}=p_i(S_0)_{jk}+p_j(S_0)_{ki}+p_k(S_0)_{ij}
          +p_ip_jp_k.$$

<a id="stmt-flux-boundary-parity"></a>
<div id="flux:boundary-parity" class="lemma">

<a id="flux:boundary-contraction"></a>
**Lemma 18**. *At such a point, $$\label{flux:boundary-contraction}
 p_lG_{lijk}\frac{B_{ijk}}{w^2}
 =p_lG_{lijk}V_{ijk}
 =U_{jk}(S_0+p\otimes p)_{jk}+C_{lijk}p_lV_{ijk}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-flux-boundary-parity"><summary>Proof of stmt-flux-boundary-parity</summary>

*Proof.* Let $R=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}-2p\otimes p$ be reflection in $p^\perp$. Since $A$ is symmetric and $Ap=\lambda p$, it preserves $p^\perp$, and hence $RAR^T=A$. Also $Rp=-p$. Orthogonal equivariance and the even parity in $p$ of $U,K$, and therefore of $G$, give $$R^{\otimes4}G(A,p,0)=G(RAR^T,Rp,0)
                   =G(A,-p,0)=G(A,p,0).$$ For the three-tensor $F_{ijk}=p_lG_{lijk}$, contraction commutes with orthogonal transformations, so $$R^{\otimes3}F=(Rp)_l(R^{\otimes4}G)_{lijk}=-F.$$ Choose any orthonormal basis with its last vector $p$. A component of a three-tensor with $k$ normal slots is multiplied by $(-1)^k$ under $R^{\otimes3}$. It follows that $F$ has zero components whenever the number of normal slots is even. This assertion concerns the full unsymmetrized tensor $F$; no permutation of its slots, and no extra symmetry of $U$ or $K$, has been used.

For tangent indices $\alpha,\beta$, the boundary jet identities give $$\frac{B_{N\alpha\beta}}{w^2}
 =\lambda A_{\alpha\beta}-(A^2)_{\alpha\beta}
 =(S_0)_{\alpha\beta},\qquad
 \frac{B_{NNN}}{w^2}=1.$$ Because $S_0p=0$, these are precisely the components of $V$ with one or three normal slots, including all their permutations. Thus the part of $B/w^2$ with an odd number of normal slots equals $V$; $V$ itself has no even-normal components. The even-normal components of $B$, which are not determined by $A,p,g,w$, have zero contraction with $F$. This proves the first equality in <a href="#flux:boundary-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:boundary-contraction">[flux:boundary-contraction]</a>.

Finally, $S_0p=0$ and $|p|=1$ imply $$p_iV_{ijk}=(S_0)_{jk}+p_jp_k.$$ Splitting $G$ according to <a href="#flux:CG" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:CG">[flux:CG]</a> proves the second equality. The argument used only the orthogonal splitting $\mathbb R^5=p^\perp\oplus\mathbb Rp$. It therefore applies without any assumption on multiplicities, signs, or nonvanishing of the tangential eigenvalues of $A$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-flux-boundary-comparison"></a>
<div id="flux:boundary-comparison" class="proposition">

**Proposition 19**. *On the regular side boundary, <a id="flux:signed-boundary-identity"></a>$$\label{flux:signed-boundary-identity}
 N\cdot Z+Hh^2=w^{2a+1}\mathcal S
   \geq\delta_*w^{2a+1}>0,
 \qquad
 \delta_* =\frac{6984137359}{31250000000},$$ where $\mathcal S$ is the boundary polynomial in <a href="#cert:boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:boundary">[cert:boundary]</a>. In particular $N\cdot Z+Hh^2\geq0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-flux-boundary-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-flux-boundary-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* Proposition <a href="#cert:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:universal">15</a> gives $P=0$ at the boundary, where $g=0$. Since $X=B/w$, Lemma <a href="#flux:boundary-parity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:boundary-parity">18</a> gives $$\begin{aligned}
 N\cdot Z
 &=w^{2a+1}\left(
       U_{jk}(S_0+p\otimes p)_{jk}
       +C_{lijk}p_lV_{ijk}+p_lM_l\right),\\
 Hh^2&=-\lambda w^{2a+1}f.
\end{aligned}$$ Their sum is exactly $w^{2a+1}\mathcal S$. The boundary data satisfy all hypotheses of <a href="#cert:boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:boundary">[cert:boundary]</a>, proving the stated lower bound. The sign is expressed using the inward normal $N$; the outward normal used by the divergence theorem is $-N$.

Only the boundary value of $P$ vanishes. Its derivative term $D_lP_l$ remains in <a href="#flux:divergence-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:divergence-identity">[flux:divergence-identity]</a>. For example, a monomial $P_l=gQ_l$ has $D_iP_l=p_iQ_l+gD_iQ_l$ at $z=1$, so its derivative need not vanish when $g=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Uniform estimates for the cutoff argument

<a id="stmt-flux-field-bounds"></a>
<div id="flux:field-bounds" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20**. *There is a finite constant $C$, depending only on the coefficient polynomials, the dimension, $a$, and $\epsilon_*$, such that on $\Omega_+$ <a id="flux:uniform-field-bounds"></a>$$\label{flux:uniform-field-bounds}
 |h|\leq Cw^a,\qquad
 |\nabla h|\leq Cw^a(1+w+|X|),\qquad
 |Z|\leq Cw^{2a}(1+w+|X|).$$ The constant is independent of $w$, $|X|$, and all subsequent cutoff parameters.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-flux-field-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-flux-field-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* The coefficient domain $$\{(A,p,g):A\in\mathop{\mathrm{STF}}^2(\mathbb R^5),\ |A|=1,\ |p|^2+g^2\leq1\}$$ is compact. The coefficient polynomials and their first derivatives with respect to their coefficient variables are therefore uniformly bounded there. Also $|q|\leq|X|$ by Cauchy–Schwarz. The rules <a href="#flux:first-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:first-derivatives">[flux:first-derivatives]</a> consequently imply $$|D f|+|q|\,|f|\leq C(1+w+|X|).$$ Use <a href="#flux:f-positive" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:f-positive">[flux:f-positive]</a> and <a href="#flux:square-root-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:square-root-derivative">[flux:square-root-derivative]</a> for the estimates on $h$ and $\nabla h$, and use <a href="#flux:fields" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="flux:fields">[flux:fields]</a> and the boundedness of $G,M,P$ for the estimate on $Z$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:certificate-proof"></a>
### Exact verification of the algebraic certificate

We return to the free algebraic data of Section <a href="#sec:certificate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificate">4</a> to prove Proposition <a href="#cert:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:universal">15</a>. All tensors are again over $\mathbb R^5$. The scalars $w,z$ and vector $\ell$ are free, and $X$ is an arbitrary symmetric trace-free three-tensor. Table I specifies the polynomials $f,U,K,M,P$, and Table II specifies nonnegative comparison forms. For the interior inequality, we compare $\mathcal I$ with a positive diagonal quadratic form in $(w,z,\ell,X)$ plus projected tensor squares, and bound the remaining error term by term. For the boundary inequality, the analogous comparison uses scalar squares in the four tangential eigenvalues and a uniform bound on eleven remainder coefficients. We give the contraction rules and rational bounds in full.

<a id="cert:decode"></a>
#### Tensor monomials and the fixed polynomials

Here and in Appendix <a href="#app:tables" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:tables">9</a>, a rank-$r$ tensor monomial is encoded by a word of decimal digits, partitioned into triples. Its first triple $hbj$ specifies $$g^h(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^3)^b w^{\mathbf1_{\{j=1\}}}
                         z^{\mathbf1_{\{j=2\}}}.$$ Each later triple $uvk$ specifies an edge carrying the matrix $A^k$, with $A^0=\delta$. An endpoint labeled $i\in\{0,\ldots,r-1\}$ is the $i$th free slot, starting with slot zero. An endpoint labeled $5$ carries one copy of $p$, and one labeled $6$ carries one copy of $\ell$. Three incidences of label $7$ are contracted against the three slots of one $X$; three incidences of label $8$, if present, use a second copy of $X$. Each occurrence of a vector endpoint is a separate vector factor. Matrix entries, vector entries, and tensor entries are multiplied, and all nonfree slots are summed. Edges are unoriented because $A$ is symmetric. Permuting the three incidences at an $X$ does not change its value because $X$ is symmetric. There are no implicit multiplicities or factorials in this convention.

Write $\mathcal M_r(s)$ for the tensor encoded by the word $s$. For example, $$\mathcal M_0(\texttt{200550})=g^2|p|^2,\qquad
 \mathcal M_4(\texttt{000500520131})_{lijk}=p_lp_jA_{ik},\qquad
 \mathcal M_1(\texttt{000771700})_l=q_l.$$ The digit labels encode slots and must be distinguished from the coordinate indices that are summed from $1$ to $5$.

<a id="cert:table-I"></a>
##### Table I

For $F\in\{f,U,K,M,P\}$, let $r_F$ be respectively $0,2,4,1,1$, with slot orders respectively empty, $(j,k)$, $(l,i,j,k)$, $(l)$, $(l)$. If a row of Table I consists of $F,s,c$, its contribution is $c\mathcal M_{r_F}(s)/Q$; thus each polynomial is given by <a id="cert:fixed-polynomials"></a>$$\label{cert:fixed-polynomials}
 F=\frac1Q\sum_{(F,s,c)\ \text{in Table I}}c\,\mathcal M_{r_F}(s).$$ Table I contains $34,57,34,74,32$ rows for $f,U,K,M,P$, respectively. Every free slot occurs exactly once in every row, and these rows have no $X,\ell,w,z$ factors. Every $P$ row has a positive $g$ exponent. Because an edge has two endpoints and $U,K$ have even rank, their numbers of $p$ endpoints are even. This proves the parity assertions of Proposition <a href="#cert:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:universal">15</a> directly from the definitions. The same definitions show that all five polynomials are equivariant under orthogonal changes of coordinates, with $f$ invariant.

<a id="cert:gram"></a>
#### The nonnegative comparison polynomials

The positive tensor squares below have the form of Gram-matrix certificates for polynomial positivity; compare <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Parrilo2003](#ref-Parrilo2003), Section 3.2]. Here all rational polynomials, projection identities, and remainder bounds are explicit, and we verify the certificate directly.

<a id="cert:gram-block"></a>
A header $\texttt{@ }r\ \mathrm{kind}\ \mathrm{mode}$ in Table II begins a new block; consecutive equal headers still designate different blocks. For a block of nonnegative rank, write its monomial rows as $s_1,\ldots,s_N$ and its rectangular integer coefficient matrix as $(c_{tc})$. Define $$J_c=\frac1Q\sum_{t=1}^N c_{tc}\mathcal M_r(s_t).$$ Its contribution to $\Gamma$ is $$\label{cert:gram-block}
 \rho\sum_c\langle J_c,\Pi J_c\rangle,\qquad
 \rho=\begin{cases}1,&\mathrm{mode}=\texttt{I},\\
                    \chi,&\mathrm{mode}=\texttt{B}.
       \end{cases}$$ Here $\Pi$ is specified below by $r$ and $\mathrm{kind}$. There are nineteen such blocks with a total of $529$ monomial rows. Every row has degree one in $(w,z,\ell,X)$, assigning degree one to each component of these variables and degree zero to $A,p,g$. Hence $\Gamma$ has degree two in these variables.

<a id="cert:boundary-gram"></a>
The two remaining blocks have rank $-1$ and fourteen rows each. In these blocks the four digits of a row word are an exponent $\beta=(\beta_1,\ldots,\beta_4)$, encoding $x^\beta$ for $x\in\mathbb R^4$. Both coefficient matrices have five columns. Set $$J_c(x)=\frac1Q\sum_t c_{tc}x^{\beta_t},\qquad
 \operatorname{Av}F(x)=\frac1{24}\sum_{\pi\in S_4}
                  F(x_{\pi(1)},\ldots,x_{\pi(4)}).$$ Their sum defines the scalar polynomial $$\label{cert:boundary-gram}
 \Lambda(x)=\sum_{\text{rank }-1\text{ blocks}}
            \operatorname{Av}\left(\rho(x)\sum_c J_c(x)^2\right),
 \qquad
 \rho(x)=\begin{cases}1,&\mathrm{mode}=\texttt{I},\\
             x_1+x_2+x_3+x_4,&\mathrm{mode}=\texttt{H}.
             \end{cases}$$ Consequently $\Lambda\ge0$ when $\sum x_i\ge0$.

<a id="cert:projections"></a>
##### Scaled projection operators

<a id="cert:sym2"></a>
<a id="cert:sym3"></a>
<a id="cert:hook"></a>
For an arbitrary rank-$r$ tensor $F$, let $F^\pi$ denote slot permutation by $\pi$. We use the averaged symmetrization $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}F=(r!)^{-1}\sum_{\pi\in S_r}F^\pi$. The operator $\operatorname{Tr}_{ab}$ contracts slots $a,b$ with the Euclidean metric, leaving the remaining slots in their original order. The label $\texttt{none}$ means $\Pi=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$. The label $\texttt{anti}$ means $$\Pi F=\sum_{\pi\in S_r}\operatorname{sgn}(\pi)F^\pi.$$ For $\texttt{sym}$, put $F'=\sum_{\pi\in S_r}F^\pi$ and $v=\operatorname{Tr}_{12}F'$. The operators used in Table II are $$\begin{aligned}
 (\Pi F)_{ij}&=5F'_{ij}-\delta_{ij}v &&(r=2),\label{cert:sym2}\\
 (\Pi F)_{ijk}&=7F'_{ijk}
       -(\delta_{ij}v_k+\delta_{ik}v_j+\delta_{jk}v_i) &&(r=3).
       \label{cert:sym3}
\end{aligned}$$ For $r=3$ and $\texttt{hook}$, set $$\begin{aligned}
 F'&=3(F+F^{(12)})-\sum_{\pi\in S_3}F^\pi,
       &v&=\operatorname{Tr}_{12}F',\notag\\
 (\Pi F)_{ijk}&=8F'_{ijk}
       -(2\delta_{ij}v_k-\delta_{ik}v_j-\delta_{jk}v_i).
       \label{cert:hook}
\end{aligned}$$

<a id="stmt-cert-positive-projections"></a>
<div id="cert:positive-projections" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21**. *All these operators are self-adjoint and positive semidefinite on their entire tensor spaces. In particular, $\Gamma\ge0$ whenever $\chi\ge0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cert-positive-projections"><summary>Proof of stmt-cert-positive-projections</summary>

*Proof.* Each slot permutation is orthogonal, with adjoint its inverse. Averaging over all permutations, with or without their signs, gives the orthogonal projection onto symmetric or alternating tensors. Thus $\texttt{anti}$ is $r!$ times an orthogonal projection. For a symmetric matrix $S$, $S-\delta\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}S/5$ is its orthogonal trace-free projection. Since $F'=2\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}F$ in <a href="#cert:sym2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:sym2">[cert:sym2]</a>, that operator is ten times the symmetric trace-free projection.

For rank three define $$(\mathcal Jv)_{ijk}=\delta_{ij}v_k+\delta_{ik}v_j+\delta_{jk}v_i.$$ Then $\operatorname{Tr}_{12}\mathcal Jv=7v$, and for symmetric $S$, $\langle S,\mathcal Jv\rangle=3\langle\operatorname{Tr}_{12}S,v\rangle$. It follows that $S-\mathcal J(\operatorname{Tr}_{12}S)/7$ is the orthogonal projection onto symmetric trace-free tensors.
