Since now $F'=6\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}F$, <a href="#cert:sym3" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:sym3">[cert:sym3]</a> is $42$ times that projection.

For completeness, the trace correction in <a href="#cert:hook" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:hook">[cert:hook]</a> can be checked without any representation-theoretic assumptions. Let $$S_{12}=\frac{\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}+(12)}2,\qquad
 S=\frac16\sum_{\pi\in S_3}\pi,\qquad P_0=S_{12}-S.$$ The identities $S_{12}S=SS_{12}=S$ show that $P_0$ is an orthogonal projection. Its image $\mathcal W$ consists of first-pair symmetric tensors orthogonal to fully symmetric tensors. For $H\in\mathcal W$, $$H_{ijk}=H_{jik},\qquad H_{ijk}+H_{ikj}+H_{jki}=0.$$ If $v=\operatorname{Tr}_{12}H$, contraction of the second identity gives $\operatorname{Tr}_{13}H=\operatorname{Tr}_{23}H=-v/2$. Define $$(\mathcal Bv)_{ijk}=2\delta_{ij}v_k-\delta_{ik}v_j-\delta_{jk}v_i.$$ Direct substitution gives $\mathcal Bv\in\mathcal W$, $\operatorname{Tr}_{12}\mathcal Bv=8v$, and $$\langle H,\mathcal Bz\rangle
 =2\langle v,z\rangle
  -\langle\operatorname{Tr}_{13}H,z\rangle
  -\langle\operatorname{Tr}_{23}H,z\rangle
 =3\langle v,z\rangle.$$ Therefore $Q_0H=H-\mathcal B(\operatorname{Tr}_{12}H)/8$ has all traces zero, and $H-Q_0H$ is orthogonal to every trace-free tensor in $\mathcal W$. Thus $Q_0$ is the orthogonal projection in $\mathcal W$ onto its trace-free part. Since $F'=6P_0F$, the hook operator is $48Q_0P_0$; $Q_0P_0$ is the orthogonal projection in the full tensor space onto that subspace. In particular $$\langle F,\Pi F\rangle=48|Q_0P_0F|^2\ge0.$$ Finally the weights in <a href="#cert:gram-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:gram-block">[cert:gram-block]</a> are nonnegative under the stated constraint. ◻

</details>

<a id="cert:collection"></a>
#### Exact contraction and differentiation rules

We give explicit collection rules both to specify the finite calculation and to justify that it is an identity on all the constrained tensors. A graph key records the rank, powers of $g,w,z$, a multiset of trace factors $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^k$, and a multiset of edges with their powers. For purposes of ordering keys, replace endpoint labels $5,6,7,8$ by $-1,-2,-3,-4$. Sort the endpoints of each edge, sort the edge list and trace list, and take the lexicographically larger edge list obtained by retaining or exchanging $-3,-4$. A lone cubic tensor is thereby labeled $-3$. These choices are only ordering conventions: exchange of two identical copies of $X$ preserves the value of every graph.

Tensor multiplication takes the disjoint union of graphs, shifts the free slot labels of the second factor, and distinguishes any two cubic tensor copies. A contraction of two free slots joins their incident edges. If these are different edges, their powers add; if they are the same edge, it becomes a trace $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^k$. The removed free slots are deleted and the others renumbered in order. The identities underlying these operations are precisely $$\sum_i(A^h)_{ai}(A^k)_{ib}=(A^{h+k})_{ab},\qquad
 \sum_i(A^k)_{ii}=\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^k.$$ Reduce the trace factors of powers $0,1,2$ to $5,0,1$, respectively. An edge of power zero connecting two slots of the same $X$ makes the monomial zero. Thus every reduction is an equality on the allowed data. Other identities need not be imposed: retaining two different keys for functions that happen to agree cannot invalidate either the expansion or the later triangle inequality.

The derivative of a path and of a trace is <a id="cert:path-derivative"></a><a id="cert:trace-derivative"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 D_l(A^k)&=\sum_{h=0}^{k-1}A^hX_lA^{k-1-h}-kq_lA^k,
       & (X_l)_{ij}&=X_{lij},\label{cert:path-derivative}\\
 D_l\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^k&=k\,\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}(A^{k-1}X_l)-kq_l\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^k.
       \label{cert:trace-derivative}
\end{aligned}$$ For $k=0$ the sums and derivatives are zero. These formulas follow by differentiating each matrix factor using <a href="#cert:D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:D">[cert:D]</a>; cyclic invariance of trace makes all $k$ insertion positions on a trace equal. In graph form, inserting $X_l$ on an edge of power $k$ splits it into two paths of lengths $h,k-1-h$ ending at a new $X$, whose last slot is the derivative index $l$. The negative terms from all $A$ factors combine into minus the total $A$ degree times $q_l$ times the original graph. Each $p$ endpoint is in turn replaced by $wA_{l\cdot}-gz\delta_{l\cdot}$, and a factor $g^h$ gives $hzg^{h-1}p_l$. If an edge has two $p$ endpoints, these are two product-rule terms, including their multiplicity when they coincide.

These operations are also sound if traces were simplified first, since $$D_l\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A=0,\qquad
 D_l\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^2=2X_{lij}A_{ij}-2q_l|A|^2=0.$$ Only the original Table I monomials are differentiated, exactly once. No already differentiated graph, or graph involving $X,w,z$, is differentiated again. Thus no unspecified higher derivative enters the calculation.

<a id="stmt-cert-closure"></a>
<div id="cert:closure" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22** (Closure of the graph calculation). *The preceding rules evaluate all terms of $\mathcal I$ and $\Gamma$ by a finite sum of scalar graphs. In every intermediate graph a free slot has exactly one incidence, each cubic tensor has exactly three incidences, and there are at most two cubic tensors. Every resulting scalar graph has degree two in $(w,z,\ell,X)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cert-closure"><summary>Proof of stmt-cert-closure</summary>

*Proof.* The incidence assertions hold for each table row by its decoding. A disjoint union preserves them. A permutation only renames free slots. Contracting two free slots removes their unique incidences and joins the remaining ends, or closes a trace; it does not change the number of slots of any cubic tensor. The derivative insertion described above replaces one edge by two incidences of a new $X$ and supplies its third incidence as a new free slot. Trace differentiation does the same with a self-edge. The $q_l$ term supplies two incidences on its self-edge and the third at $l$. Derivatives of $p$ and $g$ supply a free derivative slot without introducing a cubic tensor. These statements prove the incidence assertions by induction over the operations.

Table I has degree zero and its derivative has degree one in the indicated variables. The formula for $\mathcal I$ multiplies such derivatives by one further factor, or a degree-zero coefficient by two factors. Each Table II row has degree one; projection, contraction, and multiplication by $\chi$ preserve that degree. Squaring produces degree two. In particular no operation used here needs three copies of $X$. Termination is immediate from the finite tables, finite permutation sums, and the finite product rule; every contraction reduces the number of free slots by two. ◻

</details>

The linear contribution of each Table I row is obtained by substitution into <a href="#cert:interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:interior">[cert:interior]</a>, forming $C=K-K^{(12)}$ for a $K$ row. One check on the slot order and coefficients is the single row $U=A$ with coefficient one. Its interior contribution is $$5z^2+|X|^2+(2a-2)|q|^2,$$ and its boundary contribution is $2\lambda-\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^3$. To expand a Gram block, sum over unordered pairs of rows $s,t$: the coefficient is $\sum_c c_{sc}c_{tc}/Q^2$, doubled when $s\ne t$, and the graph polynomial is the contraction of row $t$ with $\Pi$ applied to row $s$, multiplied by $\rho$. This uses self-adjointness of $\Pi$ and accounts for every term of <a href="#cert:gram-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:gram-block">[cert:gram-block]</a> exactly once.

<a id="cert:bounds"></a>
#### Bounds for scalar graphs

Put $\theta=4/5$. We first establish the tensor estimates that will bound each scalar graph, retaining the dependence between $p$ and $g$.

<a id="stmt-cert-tensor-bounds"></a>
<div id="cert:tensor-bounds" class="lemma">

<a id="cert:trace-bounds"></a>
<a id="cert:five-sevenths"></a>
<a id="cert:mixed-bound"></a>
**Lemma 23**. *For the constrained matrix $A$ and symmetric trace-free tensor $X$, <a id="cert:matrix-bounds"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \|A\|_{\mathrm{op}}^2&\le\theta,&
 |A^k|^2&\le\theta^{k-1}\quad(k\ge1),\label{cert:matrix-bounds}\\
 |\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^k|^2&\le\theta^{k-2}\quad(k\ge2),&
 |\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^3|^2&\le\frac9{20},\label{cert:trace-bounds}\\
 |X:A|^2&\le\frac57|X|^2,&
 (X:A)_i&=X_{ijk}A_{jk}.\label{cert:five-sevenths}
\end{aligned}$$ If $h,b>0$ and $g^2+|p|^2\le1$, then $$\label{cert:mixed-bound}
 |g|^{2h}|p|^{2b}
 \le \left(\frac{h}{h+b}\right)^h
       \left(\frac{b}{h+b}\right)^b.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cert-tensor-bounds"><summary>Proof of stmt-cert-tensor-bounds</summary>

*Proof.* Let the eigenvalues of $A$ be $\alpha_1,\ldots,\alpha_5$. For each $i$, $$\alpha_i^2=\left(\sum_{j\ne i}\alpha_j\right)^2
       \le4\sum_{j\ne i}\alpha_j^2=4(1-\alpha_i^2).$$ This proves the operator bound, and $\sum_i\alpha_i^{2k}\le\theta^{k-1}\sum_i\alpha_i^2$ proves the Frobenius bound. Moreover $|\sum_i\alpha_i^k|\le\sum_i|\alpha_i|^k
\le\theta^{(k-2)/2}\sum_i\alpha_i^2$ for $k\ge2$.

For the sharper cubic bound maximize and minimize $\sum_i\alpha_i^3$ on the compact set $\sum_i\alpha_i=0$, $\sum_i\alpha_i^2=1$. The gradients of the two constraints are independent there. Lagrange multipliers give $3\alpha_i^2=\mu+2\nu\alpha_i$, so an extremum has two distinct eigenvalues (a single value would violate the constraints). If their multiplicities are $j,5-j$, the values are, up to simultaneous sign, $$\sqrt{\frac{5-j}{5j}},\qquad
 -\sqrt{\frac{j}{5(5-j)}}.$$ Their cubic trace has absolute value $|5-2j|/\sqrt{5j(5-j)}$. Its square is $9/20$ for $j=1,4$ and $1/30$ for $j=2,3$, proving <a href="#cert:trace-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:trace-bounds">[cert:trace-bounds]</a>.

To prove <a href="#cert:five-sevenths" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:five-sevenths">[cert:five-sevenths]</a>, fix a vector $\zeta$ and symmetrize $\zeta\otimes A$: $$B_{ijk}=\frac{\zeta_iA_{jk}+\zeta_jA_{ik}+\zeta_kA_{ij}}3,
 \qquad v=\operatorname{Tr}_{12}B=\frac23 A\zeta.$$ Expanding the nine terms in $|B|^2$ gives $|B|^2=|\zeta|^2/3+2|A\zeta|^2/3$. The symmetric trace-free projection is $F=B-\mathcal Jv/7$, in the notation of Lemma <a href="#cert:positive-projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:positive-projections">21</a>. Since $|\mathcal Jv|^2=21|v|^2$ and $\langle B,\mathcal Jv\rangle=3|v|^2$, $$|F|^2=|B|^2-\frac37|v|^2
       =\frac13|\zeta|^2+\frac{10}{21}|A\zeta|^2
       \le\frac57|\zeta|^2.$$ Symmetry and trace-freeness of $X$ give $\langle X:A,\zeta\rangle=\langle X,F\rangle$; Cauchy–Schwarz and duality prove the claim.

Finally put $s=g^2,t=|p|^2$. For $h,b>0$, the maximum of $s^ht^b$ on $s,t\ge0$, $s+t\le1$ lies on $s+t=1$. Differentiating $h\log s+b\log(1-s)$ gives the unique interior maximum $s=h/(h+b)$, proving <a href="#cert:mixed-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:mixed-bound">[cert:mixed-bound]</a>. If either exponent is zero, the corresponding bound is simply $1$. ◻

</details>

For a nonzero scalar graph key $t$ of degree two, let $$y=(|w|,|z|,|\ell|,|X|)^T$$ and let $i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\}$ be its two degree-one types, counted with multiplicity and ordered so $i\le j$. Let $h$ be its $g$ exponent and $b$ its number of $p$ endpoints. Define $L_t^2$ as a product of the following rational factors:

1.  <span id="cert:graph-rule-trace" label="cert:graph-rule-trace"></span> for each trace $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^k$, use $\theta^{k-2}$, replacing this by $9/20$ when $k=3$;

2.  <span id="cert:graph-rule-edge" label="cert:graph-rule-edge"></span> for an edge of power $k$ between distinct vertices, use $\theta^k$;

3.  <span id="cert:graph-rule-self" label="cert:graph-rule-self"></span> for an edge between two slots of the same $X$, use $5/7$ when $k=1$ and $\theta^{k-1}$ when $k\ge2$;

4.  <span id="cert:graph-rule-mixed" label="cert:graph-rule-mixed"></span> if $h,b>0$, include the right side of <a href="#cert:mixed-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:mixed-bound">[cert:mixed-bound]</a>; otherwise include $1$.

Here separate occurrences of a vector endpoint count as distinct vertices; thus a $p$–$p$ edge is governed by Item <a href="#cert:graph-rule-edge" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:graph-rule-edge">[cert:graph-rule-edge]</a>, not Item <a href="#cert:graph-rule-self" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:graph-rule-self">[cert:graph-rule-self]</a>. Power-zero self-edges at $X$ have already vanished in the collection rules.

<a id="stmt-cert-graph-bound"></a>
<div id="cert:graph-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 24** (Complete graph bound). *Every scalar graph retained by Lemma <a href="#cert:closure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:closure">22</a> satisfies <a id="cert:monomial-bound"></a>$$\label{cert:monomial-bound}
 |\mathcal M(t)|\le L_t y_i y_j$$ on $g^2+|p|^2\le1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cert-graph-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-cert-graph-bound</summary>

*Proof.* First separate all scalar trace factors and use Lemma <a href="#cert:tensor-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:tensor-bounds">23</a>. A cubic tensor has at most one self-edge, since such an edge consumes two of its three incidences. An edge of power $k\ge2$ reduces that tensor to the vector $v_i=X_{ijk}(A^k)_{jk}$, for which $|v|\le|X|\,|A^k|\le|X|\theta^{(k-1)/2}$. For $k=1$ use <a href="#cert:five-sevenths" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:five-sevenths">[cert:five-sevenths]</a>. These reductions leave at most two cubic vertices and no self-edge on either.

We list all possible connected components of the remaining graph. With zero cubic vertices, a component is one edge joining two vectors; its contraction is bounded by the operator norm of its matrix times the two vector norms. With one cubic vertex, all its three slots end at vector leaves. Move each edge matrix onto its vector, and use tensor Cauchy–Schwarz, obtaining the norm of $X$ times the three vector norms and the product of the three operator norms.

If a component contains two cubic vertices, they are joined by $r=1,2,$ or $3$ edges; every other slot ends at a vector leaf. Contract these leaves first, obtaining rank-$r$ tensors $Y,Z$ with the same preceding norm bounds. Up to a permutation of their slots, the remaining contraction is $$\left\langle Y,
      (A^{k_1}\otimes\cdots\otimes A^{k_r})Z\right\rangle.$$ Its absolute value is at most $|Y||Z|\prod_{s=1}^r\|A^{k_s}\|_{\mathrm{op}}$. This includes all three parallel edges when $r=3$, without a dimension factor. If the two cubic vertices have no edge between them, they lie in two components of the one-vertex type already treated. These cases are exhaustive: all other vertices have valence one, so cannot occur internally on a path, and there are no further cubic vertices. The reduced vectors from self-edges are ordinary vector leaves in this classification.

Multiply the estimates over components. Each original $\ell$ or $X$ norm appears once per occurrence, all ordinary edge matrices contribute $\theta^{k/2}$, and the $p$ endpoints contribute $|p|^b$. The scalar factors $g^h$ and $|p|^b$ are bounded jointly by <a href="#cert:mixed-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:mixed-bound">[cert:mixed-bound]</a>. Finally the total degree is two, so all remaining $w,z,\ell,X$ factors give exactly $y_i y_j$. ◻

</details>

We can now bound every term left after subtracting the nonnegative comparison forms. The graph estimates reduce this remainder to a quadratic form in four magnitudes. The next calculation shows that its error is smaller than the reserved positive diagonal terms.

<a id="cert:interior-remainder"></a>
#### The interior remainder and its coercive margin

<a id="cert:remainders"></a>
Set $$\label{cert:remainders}
 d=\left(\frac2{25},\frac2{25},\frac14,1\right),\qquad
 E=\Gamma+\sum_{i=1}^4d_i y_i^2-\mathcal I.$$ The squared norms in Equation <a href="#cert:remainders" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:remainders">[cert:remainders]</a> are polynomial contractions. Expand and collect $E=\sum_t e_t\mathcal M(t)$ by Section <a href="#cert:collection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:collection">6.3</a>, discarding zero coefficients only after their signed contributions have been summed. All coefficients are rational. The denominator $Q^2$ suffices: Gram terms have that denominator, and the denominators of $a,d_i$, and all Table I coefficients divide it after multiplication by $Q^2$.

<a id="cert:root-rounding"></a>
<a id="cert:D0"></a>
<a id="cert:error-bound"></a>
For each retained key let $n_t$ be the least nonnegative integer satisfying $$\label{cert:root-rounding}
 n_t^2\ge10^{10}L_t^2,
 \qquad \widehat L_t=\frac{n_t}{10^5}.$$ There is no floating-point square root in this prescription. For a rational $s=10^{10}L_t^2$, compute $n=\lfloor\sqrt{\lfloor s\rfloor}\rfloor$ by integer arithmetic and increase it by one exactly when $n^2<s$. Form a symmetric matrix $D_0$ by adding $$\label{cert:D0}
 \frac{|e_t|\widehat L_t}{1+\mathbf1_{\{i\ne j\}}}$$ to its $ij$ entry and also its $ji$ entry if $i\ne j$, starting with zero. The factor $1/2$ in an off-diagonal entry is required because the associated term in $y^TD_0y$ is $2(D_0)_{ij}y_iy_j$. Lemma <a href="#cert:graph-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:graph-bound">24</a> and the triangle inequality therefore give $$\label{cert:error-bound}
 |E|\le y^TD_0y.$$

<a id="cert:exact-interior-sums"></a>
For clarity, the exact finite calculation needed here is the following. Decode all rows of Tables I and II; expand the nineteen Gram blocks by unordered row pairs and the finite projections <a href="#cert:sym2" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:sym2">[cert:sym2]</a>–<a href="#cert:hook" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:hook">[cert:hook]</a>; subtract each Table I contribution to <a href="#cert:interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:interior">[cert:interior]</a>; and add the four contractions with coefficients $d_i$. Use the contraction, derivative, and key ordering rules above after each operation. Combine all signed coefficients at equal keys. For each nonzero key compute its rational $L_t^2$, perform <a href="#cert:root-rounding" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:root-rounding">[cert:root-rounding]</a>, and add <a href="#cert:D0" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:D0">[cert:D0]</a>. This gives all ten upper-triangular entries as follows: $$\label{cert:exact-interior-sums}
\begin{array}{c|r|r}
 (i,j)&\text{nonzero keys}&2\cdot10^5Q^2(D_0)_{ij}\\\hline
 (1,1)&89&321268243322644\\
 (1,2)&53&96616799870252\\
 (1,3)&123&294707669526536\\
 (1,4)&170&385792585960684\\
 (2,2)&64&269583307398596\\
 (2,3)&85&115717471031956\\
 (2,4)&106&125155603107368\\
 (3,3)&238&605509501361296\\
 (3,4)&414&618424270356936\\
 (4,4)&372&1004504025266456
\end{array}$$ In particular there are $1714$ nonzero keys. These finite sums use only integer and rational operations and comparisons; the complete literal input is in Appendix <a href="#app:tables" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:tables">9</a>. The accompanying program `verification/checks/reproduce.py` implements the specified calculation and records the individual coefficients as well as these sums.

<a id="cert:upper-matrix"></a>
<a id="cert:slacks"></a>
Entrywise, <a href="#cert:exact-interior-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:exact-interior-sums">[cert:exact-interior-sums]</a> implies $D_0\le\overline D$, where $$\label{cert:upper-matrix}
 \overline D=\frac1{10^5}
 \begin{pmatrix}
 2571&773&2358&3087\\
 773&2157&926&1002\\
 2358&926&4845&4948\\
 3087&1002&4948&8037
 \end{pmatrix}.$$ For example, this entrywise check consists of comparing each last-column integer in <a href="#cert:exact-interior-sums" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:exact-interior-sums">[cert:exact-interior-sums]</a> with the corresponding integer in <a href="#cert:upper-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:upper-matrix">[cert:upper-matrix]</a> multiplied by $2Q^2$. Let $\nu=(2484,1440,996,304)^T$. The exact diagonal slacks are $$\label{cert:slacks}
 \left(d_i-\frac{(\overline D\nu)_i}{\nu_i}\right)_{i=1}^4
 =\left(\frac{673}{18400},\frac{438907}{12000000},
         \frac{2844869}{24900000},\frac{3479389}{7600000}\right).$$ For $y_i\ge0$, weighted Young inequalities give $$2y_iy_j\le\frac{\nu_j}{\nu_i}y_i^2+
                  \frac{\nu_i}{\nu_j}y_j^2,
 \qquad
 y^TD_0y\le y^T\overline D y
       \le\sum_i\frac{(\overline D\nu)_i}{\nu_i}y_i^2.$$ Use $\Gamma\ge0$ in <a href="#cert:remainders" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:remainders">[cert:remainders]</a> and then <a href="#cert:error-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:error-bound">[cert:error-bound]</a>. It follows that $\mathcal I$ is at least the sum of $y_i^2$ times the four positive slacks in <a href="#cert:slacks" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:slacks">[cert:slacks]</a>. In particular the coefficients of $z^2$ and $|\ell|^2$ are at least $\epsilon_*$, proving <a href="#cert:interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:interior">[cert:interior]</a>. To obtain the stated positivity of $f$, keep $A,p,g$ fixed, set $X=w=z=0$, and choose $|\ell|=1$. Then $q=0$, every $D$ term is zero, and $\mathcal I=f$.

<a id="cert:boundary-remainder"></a>
#### Boundary reduction and the eleven coefficients

<a id="cert:eigenvalues"></a>
<a id="cert:boundary-components"></a>
It remains to verify <a href="#cert:boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:boundary">[cert:boundary]</a>. Orthogonal equivariance allows us to take $p=e_5$ and to diagonalize $A$ on $p^\perp$. Write $$\label{cert:eigenvalues}
 A=\operatorname{diag}(x_1,x_2,x_3,x_4,\lambda),\qquad
 \lambda=-\sum_{i=1}^4x_i,
 \qquad R(x):=\sum_{i=1}^4x_i^2+\left(\sum_{i=1}^4x_i\right)^2=1.$$ No sign or distinctness condition is imposed on the $x_i$; the normal eigenvalue condition is exactly $\sum_i x_i>0$. In these coordinates $(S_0)_{55}=0$ and $(S_0)_{\alpha\alpha}=b_\alpha:=\lambda x_\alpha-x_\alpha^2$. The only nonzero entries of $V$ are $V_{555}=1$ and the three permutations of $V_{5\alpha\alpha}=b_\alpha$. Since $C_{55jk}=0$, a direct component formula for the boundary polynomial is therefore $$\label{cert:boundary-components}
 \mathcal S=-\lambda f+U_{55}+M_5+
       \sum_{\alpha=1}^4 b_\alpha
          (U_{\alpha\alpha}+C_{5\alpha5\alpha}
                                      +C_{5\alpha\alpha5}),$$ where the fixed polynomials are evaluated at <a href="#cert:eigenvalues" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:eigenvalues">[cert:eigenvalues]</a>, $p=e_5,g=0$. This formula does not assume additional slot symmetries of $U$ or $K$.

<a id="cert:boundary-substitution"></a>
Equivalently, one may contract the boundary expression as a scalar graph and then substitute $$\label{cert:boundary-substitution}
 p\cdot A^kp=\left(-\sum_i x_i\right)^k,\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^k=\sum_i x_i^k+\left(-\sum_i x_i\right)^k.$$ For $k=0$, the latter is $5$ and the former is $1$. The trace reductions $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A=0$, $\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^2=1$ remain valid on $R=1$. Thus either <a href="#cert:boundary-components" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:boundary-components">[cert:boundary-components]</a> or <a href="#cert:boundary-substitution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:boundary-substitution">[cert:boundary-substitution]</a> evaluates the same scalar on all the allowed data. Orthogonal invariance under permutations of $p^\perp$ shows that $\mathcal S$ is symmetric in $x_1,\ldots,x_4$.

Define the boundary remainder on this constraint set by $$E_b=\Lambda+\frac14-\mathcal S.$$ Expanding the two boundary Gram blocks and the Table I expression gives a polynomial representative with monomials of degree at most five. A finite way to check the degree assertion is to use <a href="#cert:boundary-components" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:boundary-components">[cert:boundary-components]</a>: discard positive $g$ powers, substitute the diagonal matrix and normal vector in each of the $231$ rows, and add the displayed terms. All remaining monomials have degree at most five. Each boundary $J_c$ has degrees one and two, so its squared terms have degree at most four, or five after the $\texttt{H}$ weight.

<a id="cert:homogenization"></a>
The following convention gives a small exact list of its coefficients. For an ordinary monomial $x^\alpha$ of degree $j\le5$, replace it by $$\label{cert:homogenization}
 x^\alpha R^{\lfloor(5-j)/2\rfloor}.$$ This preserves its value on $R=1$ and gives degree four if $j$ is even and degree five if $j$ is odd. Perform the replacement before collecting exponents. Because $R$ is symmetric, it commutes with permutation averaging. For a nondecreasing exponent vector $\beta$ put $$m_\beta(x)=\operatorname{Av}(x^\beta).$$ Expand <a href="#cert:homogenization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:homogenization">[cert:homogenization]</a> and sum the coefficients of all exponent vectors whose nondecreasing rearrangement is $\beta$. Their sum is the coefficient of $m_\beta$ after averaging: indeed $\operatorname{Av}(x^\alpha)=m_\beta$ for every member $\alpha$ of that orbit, including when exponents repeat. Thus sorting exponents and summing coefficients introduces no extra factor of $24$. Since $\mathcal S$ and the constant are symmetric and $\Lambda$ was defined by averaging, the resulting averaged polynomial still equals $E_b$ on $R=1$.

<a id="cert:boundary-expansion"></a>
Precisely, using the row-pair expansion of Section <a href="#cert:collection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:collection">6.3</a> for the two rank-$-1$ blocks, subtracting the boundary contributions from Table I with denominator $Q$, and adding the homogenized constant $1/4$, one obtains $$\label{cert:boundary-expansion}
 E_b(x)=\sum_\beta e^{(b)}_\beta m_\beta(x)\quad\text{on }R=1,$$ with exactly the following eleven nonzero coefficients: <a id="cert:eleven-coefficients"></a>$$\label{cert:eleven-coefficients}
\begin{array}{c|r@{\qquad}c|r}
 \beta&Q^2 e^{(b)}_\beta&\beta&Q^2 e^{(b)}_\beta\\\hline
 (0,0,0,4)&-13479110 &(0,0,0,5)&17712712\\
 (0,0,1,3)&-93201124 &(0,0,1,4)&151038620\\
 (0,0,2,2)&-78543848 &(0,0,2,3)&244636976\\
 (0,1,1,2)&-139986162&(0,1,1,3)&310091962\\
 (0,1,2,2)&445177656 &(1,1,1,1)&-4861942\\
 (1,1,1,2)&157995170&&
\end{array}$$ This coefficient computation consists solely of the explicitly stated substitutions, multiplication of ordinary four-variable polynomials, and coefficient addition. It is also included in the accompanying exact reproducer.

On $R=1$ every $|x_i|\le1$, hence $|m_\beta(x)|\le1$. Adding the eleven absolute integer coefficients gives $$Q^2\sum_\beta|e^{(b)}_\beta|=1656725282.$$ Since $\sum_i x_i=-\lambda>0$, <a href="#cert:boundary-gram" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:boundary-gram">[cert:boundary-gram]</a> is nonnegative. It follows from <a href="#cert:boundary-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:boundary-expansion">[cert:boundary-expansion]</a> that $$\mathcal S=\Lambda+\frac14-E_b
 \ge\frac14-\frac{1656725282}{62500000000}
 =\frac{6984137359}{31250000000}=\delta_*.$$ This completes the proof of Proposition <a href="#cert:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:universal">15</a>. The estimate also persists at $\lambda=0$ by continuity; repeated tangent eigenvalues cause no singularity in any of the formulas.

The pointwise comparisons and field bounds of Section <a href="#sec:flux" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:flux">[sec:flux]</a> are therefore established. Section <a href="#sec:cutoffs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:cutoffs">[sec:cutoffs]</a> now justifies their integration across Hessian zeros and near the suspension tips.

<a id="cut:section"></a>
### Hessian zeros, suspension tips, and the contradiction

<span id="sec:cutoffs" label="sec:cutoffs"></span>

We now justify the integration of the pointwise comparison. Retain the original smooth component $\Omega_o\subset S^{m_o}$, with $m_o\in\{4,5\}$, from Proposition <a href="#red:original-link" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:original-link">6</a> and Lemma <a href="#red:nonflat-component" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:nonflat-component">7</a>. Its tensor $T_o$ is not identically zero. Write $$B_o=\nabla T_o,\qquad w_o=|T_o|,\qquad
 \mathcal Z_o=\{y\in\overline\Omega_o:w_o(y)=0\},\qquad
 \Omega_{o,+}=\Omega_o\setminus\mathcal Z_o.$$ The tensor $T_o$ is smooth on the compact closure; its norm $w_o$ is continuous there and smooth where it is positive. The working domain $\Omega\subset S^5$ is $\Omega_o$ when $m_o=5$, and its suspension when $m_o=4$. On the regular part of this working domain we use the unadorned notation of Sections <a href="#sec:geometry" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:geometry">[sec:geometry]</a> and <a href="#sec:flux" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:flux">[sec:flux]</a>. In particular, $Z$ denotes the flux vector field, whereas $\mathcal Z_o$ denotes the original Hessian zero set. Set $M_o=\|w_o\|_{L^\infty(\Omega_o)}$. We fix $$a=\frac{29}{50},\qquad
 \epsilon_* =\frac{438907}{12000000}>0.$$ Interior and boundary integrals use their respective spherical volume and area measures; in the suspension, boundary integrals refer to its regular side boundary.

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Weighted integrability on the original component

<a id="stmt-cut-weighted-bochner"></a>
<div id="cut:weighted-bochner" class="lemma">

<a id="cut:weighted-integrability"></a>
<a id="cut:original-dominator"></a>
**Lemma 25** (Weighted Bochner estimate). *The set $\mathcal Z_o$ is a compact subset of $\Omega_o$, and $$\label{cut:weighted-integrability}
 \int_{\Omega_{o,+}}w_o^{2a-2}|B_o|^2\,dV_o<\infty.$$ Consequently the function $$\label{cut:original-dominator}
 D_o(y)=
 \begin{cases}
  w_o(y)^{2a}\bigl(1+|B_o(y)|/w_o(y)\bigr)^2,
       &y\in\Omega_{o,+},\\
  0,&y\in\mathcal Z_o,
 \end{cases}$$ belongs to $L^1(\Omega_o)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cut-weighted-bochner"><summary>Proof of stmt-cut-weighted-bochner</summary>

*Proof.* The boundary conclusion of Lemma <a href="#geo:gradient-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:gradient-bound">10</a> gives $w_o>0$ at every point of $\partial\Omega_o$. Compactness and continuity therefore give a boundary neighborhood on which $w_o$ has a positive lower bound. The closed set $\mathcal Z_o$ is thus compactly contained in the interior. If it is empty, $w_o$ has a positive minimum on the whole compact closure, and <a href="#cut:weighted-integrability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:weighted-integrability">[cut:weighted-integrability]</a> follows immediately from the boundedness of $B_o$.

<a id="cut:absorption"></a>
Otherwise choose $\eta\in C_c^\infty(\Omega_o)$ with $0\leq\eta\leq1$, equal to one on an open neighborhood of $\mathcal Z_o$. For $0<\delta\leq1$ put $W_\delta=w_o^2+\delta$. Although $w_o$ need not be smooth at its zeros, $W_\delta$ is smooth and strictly positive. The identities in Proposition <a href="#geo:interior-identities" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:interior-identities">9</a> give $$\Delta W_\delta=2m_o w_o^2+2|B_o|^2\geq2|B_o|^2,
 \qquad
 |\nabla W_\delta|^2
 =4|\langle T_o,B_o\rangle|^2
 \leq4W_\delta|B_o|^2.$$ Set $I_\delta=\int_{\Omega_o}\eta W_\delta^{a-1}|B_o|^2\,dV_o$. Applying the chain rule to $W_\delta^a$ and integrating twice by parts against the compactly supported $\eta$ yields $$\begin{aligned}
 2I_\delta
 &\leq \int_{\Omega_o}\eta W_\delta^{a-1}\Delta W_\delta\,dV_o\\
 &=\frac1a\int_{\Omega_o}(\Delta\eta)W_\delta^a\,dV_o
   +(1-a)\int_{\Omega_o}
       \eta W_\delta^{a-2}|\nabla W_\delta|^2\,dV_o\\
 &\leq\frac1a\int_{\Omega_o}(\Delta\eta)W_\delta^a\,dV_o
       +4(1-a)I_\delta.
\end{aligned}$$ Since $2-4(1-a)=4a-2=8/25$, this proves the explicit uniform estimate $$\label{cut:absorption}
 \frac8{25}I_\delta
 \leq \frac1a(M_o^2+1)^a\|\Delta\eta\|_{L^1(\Omega_o)}.$$ As $\delta\downarrow0$, the nonnegative integrands restricted to $\Omega_{o,+}$ increase to $\eta w_o^{2a-2}|B_o|^2$ because $a<1$. Monotone convergence and <a href="#cut:absorption" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:absorption">[cut:absorption]</a> control that integral. The compact set $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}(1-\eta)\subset\overline\Omega_o$ is disjoint from $\mathcal Z_o$; writing $b_\eta>0$ for the minimum of $w_o$ there, the remaining integral is bounded by $$\int_{\Omega_{o,+}}(1-\eta)w_o^{2a-2}|B_o|^2\,dV_o
 \leq b_\eta^{2a-2}\|B_o\|_{L^2(\Omega_o)}^2.$$ Thus all constants in this argument depend only on the fixed original component and its smooth data, on $a$, and on the chosen interior cutoff $\eta$, and are independent of $\delta$. Finally, on $\Omega_{o,+}$, $$D_o\leq2w_o^{2a}+2w_o^{2a-2}|B_o|^2.$$ The first term is integrable by compactness, and the second by <a href="#cut:weighted-integrability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:weighted-integrability">[cut:weighted-integrability]</a>. No measure, dimension, or regularity property of $\mathcal Z_o$ has been used. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Smooth angular cutoffs and the integrated comparison

Let $\vartheta\in C^\infty([0,\infty))$ satisfy $0\leq\vartheta\leq1$, $\vartheta=0$ on $[0,1]$, and $\vartheta=1$ on $[2,\infty)$. Fix this profile once and for all, and set $$L_\vartheta=\|\vartheta'\|_\infty,
 \qquad \kappa=2L_\vartheta.$$ For integers $j\geq3$ define <a id="cut:cutoffs"></a>$$\label{cut:cutoffs}
 \alpha_j(y)=\vartheta(jw_o(y)),\qquad
 \xi_j=
 \begin{cases}
  \alpha_j,&m_o=5,\\
  \alpha_j(y)\beta_j(t),\quad
       \beta_j(t)=\vartheta(j\sin t),&m_o=4.
 \end{cases}$$ Here $\alpha_j$ is defined to be zero at $\mathcal Z_o$, consistently with the displayed formula. It is smooth on $\overline\Omega_o$: near any zero of $w_o$ it is identically zero, and all its transitions occur where $w_o$ is smooth and positive. In the suspension, $\beta_j$ vanishes on whole neighborhoods of both tips.

Figure <a href="#fig:suspension-cutoff" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:suspension-cutoff">1</a> displays the two exclusions made by the product cutoff in the suspended case. Its transition bands shrink toward the two tips and the original Hessian zero set. The weighted estimate above will control the errors produced in all these bands at once.

<figure id="fig:suspension-cutoff">

<figcaption>The product cutoff on a suspended component. The horizontal variable is the suspension angle in $Φ(t,y) = (sint y,cost)$; the two end slices collapse to the tips $p_{+}$ and $p_{−}$. The vertical variable records the value of $w_{o}(y)$, not a coordinate on $Ω_{o}$. Gray regions have $ξ_{j} = 0$, and the blue region has $ξ_{j} = 1$; the intervening bands contain the smooth transitions. The scales are schematic, and no shape or regularity of the Hessian zero set is represented.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-suspension-cutoff"></a>

The vertical axis is labeled $w_o(y)$ with ticks at $0$, $1/j$, and $2/j$. Left vertical strip from $t=0$ to $t = \arcsin(1/j)$ is shaded gray with label $\xi_j = 0$. <a id="cut:field-estimates"></a>
On $\Omega_+=\{x\in\Omega:w(x)>0\}$, use the fields $$h=w^a\sqrt{f(A,p,g)},\qquad
 Z_l=w^{2a}(G_{lijk}X_{ijk}+wM_l+P_l)$$ constructed in Section <a href="#sec:flux" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:flux">[sec:flux]</a>. By Lemma <a href="#flux:field-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:field-bounds">20</a>, there is a fixed $C_F\geq1$ such that $$\label{cut:field-estimates}
 |h|\leq C_Fw^a,\qquad
 |\nabla h|\leq C_Fw^a(1+w+|X|),\qquad
 |Z|\leq C_Fw^{2a}(1+w+|X|).$$ The constant depends only on $a$, the dimension $5$, the finitely many coefficients defining $f,G,M,P$, and the positive lower bound $f\geq\epsilon_*$. In particular $C_F$ is independent of the cone, $w$, $X$, and $j$.

For each fixed $j$, define $v_j=\xi_jh$ and $F_j=\xi_j^2Z$ on $\Omega_+$, and extend them by zero where $w=0$. These are smooth on the regular closure of $\Omega$ and are supported away from the tips when present. The same neighborhood argument as for $\alpha_j$ proves smoothness of these extensions; it does not require $h$ or $Z$ themselves to extend smoothly across $w=0$. All products below containing one of these cutoff factors or its derivative are likewise understood to be zero on $w=0$. The mass $w^{2a}$, whose exponent is positive, has its ordinary value zero there and involves no normalized tensor.

<a id="stmt-cut-integrated-comparison"></a>
<div id="cut:integrated-comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="cut:three-errors"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Integrated comparison). *For each fixed $j\geq3$, $$\label{cut:three-errors}
 \epsilon_*\int_\Omega\xi_j^2w^{2a}\,dV
 \leq\int_\Omega\left(
    h^2|\nabla\xi_j|^2
   +2\xi_jh\langle\nabla h,\nabla\xi_j\rangle
   -2\xi_j\langle Z,\nabla\xi_j\rangle\right)\,dV.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cut-integrated-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-cut-integrated-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* The interior comparison of Proposition <a href="#flux:interior-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:interior-comparison">17</a> gives $$\int_\Omega\xi_j^2
       (4h^2+|\nabla h|^2+\epsilon_*w^{2a})\,dV
 \leq\int_\Omega\xi_j^2\mathop{\mathrm{div}}Z\,dV.$$ Apply the divergence theorem to the smooth field $F_j$. In the suspension one may first truncate the domain inside the two neighborhoods where $F_j$ vanishes; the artificial end boundaries have zero flux. On the regular side boundary the outward normal is $-N$. Thus the boundary comparison of Proposition <a href="#flux:boundary-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="flux:boundary-comparison">19</a>, $N\cdot Z+Hh^2\geq0$, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_\Omega\xi_j^2\mathop{\mathrm{div}}Z\,dV
 &=-\int_{\partial\Omega}\xi_j^2\langle N,Z\rangle\,d\sigma
       -2\int_\Omega\xi_j\langle Z,\nabla\xi_j\rangle\,dV\\
 &\leq\int_{\partial\Omega}H(\xi_jh)^2\,d\sigma
       -2\int_\Omega\xi_j\langle Z,\nabla\xi_j\rangle\,dV.
\end{aligned}$$ Corollary <a href="#geo:spherical-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:spherical-stability">14</a> applies to $v_j$ and bounds the boundary integral by $$\int_\Omega\bigl(|\nabla(\xi_jh)|^2+4\xi_j^2h^2\bigr)\,dV.$$ Expanding the square and canceling the common terms gives <a href="#cut:three-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:three-errors">[cut:three-errors]</a>. ◻

</details>

Every cancellation above is made before removing the angular cutoff. For fixed $j$ and radial index $k$, the tests $\zeta_k(r)v_j$ from Corollary <a href="#geo:spherical-stability" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:spherical-stability">14</a> have compact support on the regular Euclidean cone. First take the variation parameter $\varepsilon$ to zero on this fixed support; then let $k\to\infty$ in Equation <a href="#geo:hardy-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="geo:hardy-limit">[geo:hardy-limit]</a> to obtain the constant $4$. The smooth extension collar and the allowed variation size may depend on $j$ and $k$. We now take $j\to\infty$ only in Equation <a href="#cut:three-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:three-errors">[cut:three-errors]</a>, which contains the positive mass and three cutoff errors. The energy and boundary terms have already canceled for each fixed $j$.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Vanishing of all three errors

<a id="cut:original-cutoff-gradient"></a>
<a id="cut:tip-cutoff-gradient"></a>
On $\Omega_{o,+}$, differentiation of $\alpha_j$ and $|\nabla w_o|\leq|B_o|$ give $$\label{cut:original-cutoff-gradient}
 |\nabla_o\alpha_j|
 \leq\kappa\frac{|B_o|}{w_o}.$$ To see the constant, wherever $\vartheta'(jw_o)\ne0$ one has $1<jw_o<2$, so $j\leq2/w_o$; outside this transition region the derivative is zero. The same argument gives $$\label{cut:tip-cutoff-gradient}
 |\beta_j'(t)|\leq\frac{\kappa}{\sin t}.$$ At every fixed point of $\Omega_{o,+}$, $\alpha_j=1$ and $\nabla_o\alpha_j=0$ for all sufficiently large $j$. At every fixed $t\in(0,\pi)$ the corresponding statements hold for $\beta_j$.

Write $b_o=|B_o|/w_o$ on $\Omega_{o,+}$. Let $E_{1,j}$, $E_{2,j}$, and $E_{3,j}$ denote, in order, the three signed integrands on the right of <a href="#cut:three-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:three-errors">[cut:three-errors]</a>.

First suppose $m_o=5$, so $\Omega=\Omega_o$, and set $K_n=1+M_o$. Since $X=B_o/w_o$, $$1+w+|X|\leq K_n(1+b_o),\qquad
 |\nabla\xi_j|\leq\kappa(1+b_o).$$ Equations <a href="#cut:field-estimates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:field-estimates">[cut:field-estimates]</a> and the preceding bounds give <a id="cut:nonsuspended-domination"></a>$$\label{cut:nonsuspended-domination}
 |E_{1,j}|+|E_{2,j}|+|E_{3,j}|
 \leq C_F^2(\kappa^2+4K_n\kappa)D_o.$$ Indeed the three coefficient bounds are respectively $C_F^2\kappa^2$, $2C_F^2K_n\kappa$, and $2C_FK_n\kappa$, and $C_F\geq1$. The dominator is integrable by Lemma <a href="#cut:weighted-bochner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:weighted-bochner">25</a>. Each error is eventually zero at every point of $\Omega_{o,+}$, and is defined to be zero on $\mathcal Z_o$. Dominated convergence therefore makes the right side of <a href="#cut:three-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:three-errors">[cut:three-errors]</a> tend to zero.

<a id="cut:suspension-scaling"></a>
<a id="cut:suspended-domination"></a>
Now suppose $m_o=4$. Write $s_t=\sin t$ and use the suspension coordinates $(t,y)\in(0,\pi)\times\Omega_o$. The exact formulas in Proposition <a href="#geo:suspension-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="geo:suspension-data">12</a> give $$\label{cut:suspension-scaling}
 w(t,y)=\frac{w_o(y)}{s_t},\qquad
 |B(t,y)|^2
  =\frac{|B_o(y)|^2+3\cos^2(t)w_o(y)^2}{s_t^4},\qquad
 dV=s_t^4\,dt\,dV_o.$$ In particular $\Omega_+=(0,\pi)\times\Omega_{o,+}$, and there $$|X|=\frac{\sqrt{b_o^2+3\cos^2t}}{s_t}
       \leq\frac{\sqrt3}{s_t}(1+b_o).$$ The warped metric gives the exact cutoff identity $$|\nabla\xi_j|^2
 =\alpha_j^2|\beta_j'|^2
   +s_t^{-2}\beta_j^2|\nabla_o\alpha_j|^2,$$ so <a href="#cut:original-cutoff-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:original-cutoff-gradient">[cut:original-cutoff-gradient]</a>–<a href="#cut:tip-cutoff-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:tip-cutoff-gradient">[cut:tip-cutoff-gradient]</a> imply <a id="cut:suspended-gradient-bounds"></a>$$\label{cut:suspended-gradient-bounds}
 |\nabla\xi_j|\leq\frac{\kappa}{s_t}(1+b_o),\qquad
 1+w+|X|\leq\frac{K_s}{s_t}(1+b_o),\qquad
 K_s=1+M_o+\sqrt3.$$ Multiplying the bounds for the three errors by the volume factor in <a href="#cut:suspension-scaling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:suspension-scaling">[cut:suspension-scaling]</a> now gives $$\label{cut:suspended-domination}
 s_t^4\bigl(|E_{1,j}|+|E_{2,j}|+|E_{3,j}|\bigr)
 \leq C_F^2(\kappa^2+4K_s\kappa)
          s_t^{2-2a}D_o(y).$$ This inequality is initially read on $\Omega_{o,+}$, and both sides are assigned zero on $\mathcal Z_o$. Its constant depends only on the fixed certificate, $a$, $\vartheta$, and $M_o$, and is independent of $j$, $t$, and $y$. It also shows explicitly why the original weighted $|B_o|^2$ estimate suffices: by <a href="#cut:original-dominator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:original-dominator">[cut:original-dominator]</a>, $$s_t^{2-2a}D_o(y)
 \leq 2s_t^{21/25}
       \bigl(w_o^{29/25}+w_o^{-21/25}|B_o|^2\bigr)
 \quad\hbox{on }\Omega_{o,+}.$$ The right side is integrable with respect to $dt\,dV_o$, because $21/25>-1$ and <a href="#cut:weighted-integrability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:weighted-integrability">[cut:weighted-integrability]</a> holds. Every error is eventually zero at each fixed $(t,y)\in(0,\pi)\times\Omega_{o,+}$. Dominated convergence applied to <a href="#cut:suspended-domination" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:suspended-domination">[cut:suspended-domination]</a> therefore makes the right side of <a href="#cut:three-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:three-errors">[cut:three-errors]</a> tend to zero also in the suspension.

<a id="stmt-cut-exclusion"></a>
<div id="cut:exclusion" class="theorem">

**Theorem 27**. *The first dimension admitting a nonflat one-homogeneous global minimizer cannot be $5$ or $6$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cut-exclusion"><summary>Proof of stmt-cut-exclusion</summary>

*Proof.* If the first dimension were $5$ or $6$, the reductions would provide exactly one of the two working domains just considered. In either case, the right side of <a href="#cut:three-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:three-errors">[cut:three-errors]</a> tends to zero. On the original component, however, $$0<I_o:=\int_{\Omega_o}w_o^{2a}\,dV_o<\infty.$$ Finiteness follows from boundedness and compactness. Positivity follows from $T_o\not\equiv0$ and continuity, which give an open subset of $\Omega_o$ on which $w_o>0$. In the nonsuspended case dominated convergence gives $$\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_\Omega\xi_j^2w^{2a}\,dV=I_o.$$ In the suspended case <a href="#cut:suspension-scaling" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:suspension-scaling">[cut:suspension-scaling]</a> gives instead $$\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_\Omega\xi_j^2w^{2a}\,dV
 = I_o\int_0^\pi(\sin t)^{4-2a}\,dt
 = I_o\int_0^\pi(\sin t)^{71/25}\,dt>0.$$ Here the dominating mass density is the integrable, everywhere defined function $(\sin t)^{71/25}w_o^{29/25}$; its value is zero wherever $w_o=0$. These strictly positive limits contradict <a href="#cut:three-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cut:three-errors">[cut:three-errors]</a>, since $\epsilon_*>0$. ◻

</details>

The known lower-dimensional classification gives $d_*\geq5$, and the dimension-seven globally minimizing example gives $d_*\leq7$, as stated in Section <a href="#sec:introduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:introduction">1</a>. Theorem <a href="#cut:exclusion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cut:exclusion">27</a> excludes the only two remaining possibilities below $7$. Hence $d_*=7$, completing the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="sec:consequences"></a>
### Regularity and sharpness of the dimension bound

We now pass from the classification of homogeneous global minimizers to local free boundaries. This step uses the dimension-reduction theorem of Weiss <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Weiss1999](#ref-Weiss1999)], in the formulation <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[Velichkov2023](#ref-Velichkov2023), Theorem 1.4 and Proposition 10.13].

<details id="proof-stmt-cut-exclusion-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-cut-exclusion</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:singular-set" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:singular-set">2</a>.* Apply the cited dimension-reduction theorem with volume coefficient one on balls compactly contained in $D$. Its inputs are uniform flatness regularity, linear nondegeneracy, compactness of blowups, and the fact that all blowup limits are homogeneous global minimizers. These are the constant-coefficient results used in Proposition <a href="#red:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:regularity">3</a>; flatness regularity is <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Velichkov2023](#ref-Velichkov2023), Theorem 8.1]. The theorem gives an empty singular set below $d_*$, local finiteness in dimension $d_*$, and Hausdorff dimension at most $n-d_*$ above it. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> sets $d_*=7$. Smoothness at each regular point follows from Proposition <a href="#red:regularity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:regularity">3</a>(iii). A countable cover by interior balls gives the assertions on $D$.

For sharpness, let $U$ be the nonflat global minimizer in $\mathbb R^7$ of De Silva and Jerison <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[DeSilvaJerison2009](#ref-DeSilvaJerison2009), Theorem 1.1], and set $U_n(x,y)=U(x)$ on $\mathbb R^7\times\mathbb R^{n-7}$. Repeated application of Proposition <a href="#red:suspension" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="red:suspension">8</a> shows that $U_n$ is globally minimizing. At each point $(0,y_0)$, every rescaling of $U_n$ is $U_n$ itself, by homogeneity and translation invariance in $y$. It cannot be flat: a half-space normal would have zero $y$-component, forcing $U$ to be flat. Thus $\{0\}\times\mathbb R^{n-7}\subset\operatorname{Sing}(U_n)$. Its dimension is $n-7$, and the proved upper bound gives equality. ◻

</details>

<a id="app:tables"></a>
### Complete coefficient tables

The following two tables specify the polynomials used in Section <a href="#cert:table-I" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:table-I">6.1.1</a> and the comparison forms in Section <a href="#cert:gram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:gram">6.2</a>. Every coefficient printed below is an integer numerator with denominator $$Q=250000.$$ Leading zeroes in monomial codes are significant. All rows and all block headers are included.

<a id="app:decoding"></a>
#### Decoding and block conventions

For nonnegative rank, read a monomial code in consecutive triples. Its first triple $h\,b\,j$ contributes $g^h(\mathop{\mathrm{tr}}A^3)^b w^{[j=1]}z^{[j=2]}$. Each subsequent triple $u\,v\,k$ is an unoriented edge carrying $A^k$, with $A^0=\mathop{\mathrm{Id}}$. Endpoints $0,\ldots,r-1$ denote the free slots in order, $5$ denotes a copy of $p$, and $6$ a copy of $\ell$; three occurrences of $7$ are contracted with one $X$, and three occurrences of $8$ with a second copy. Multiply all factors and contract all nonfree slots. There are no additional multiplicity factors; see Section <a href="#cert:decode" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:decode">6.1</a>.

In Table I the first field specifies the polynomial, the second its monomial, and the third its coefficient numerator. The free-slot orders are $U_{jk}$, $K_{lijk}$, $M_l$, and $P_l$, while $f$ has rank zero. The numbers of rows for $(f,U,M,P,K)$ are $(34,57,74,32,34)$, for a total of $231$.

Each line of Table II beginning with `@` starts a separate block and gives its rank, projection label, and multiplier mode. For each coefficient column, sum the monomials with the coefficients in that column divided by $Q$ to obtain a tensor $J_c$. For nonnegative rank the block contributes $\rho\sum_c\langle J_c,\Pi J_c\rangle$ to $\Gamma$, where the modes `I` and `B` mean $\rho=1$ and $\rho=\chi=1-|p|^2-g^2$, respectively. The labels `none`, `anti`, `sym`, and `hook` specify exactly the operators of Section <a href="#cert:projections" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:projections">6.2.1</a>; they do not instruct any further normalization of the coefficients. Identical headers in different positions still define distinct blocks.

For rank $-1$, the four code digits instead give the four individual exponents of $x_1,x_2,x_3,x_4$. The block contributes to $\Lambda$ the average over all $24$ permutations of $\rho\sum_c J_c^2$. Here `I` means $\rho=1$, while `H` means $\rho=\sum_i x_i=-\lambda$. The averaging and boundary normalization $R=\sum_i x_i^2+(\sum_i x_i)^2=1$ are those of Section <a href="#cert:gram" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:gram">6.2</a>. Table II contains $21$ blocks: $19$ interior blocks with $529$ rows and two boundary blocks with $14$ rows each, hence $557$ coefficient rows in total. Each block retains its own number of coefficient columns.

<a id="app:reproduction"></a>
#### Reproduction of the finite arithmetic

In the complete source directory accompanying this article, run `make -C build check`; dependencies and the preserved calculation records are in `verification/checks/README.md`. The entry point enables assertions, fixes $a=29/50$, and checks source pins, literal table transcription, and fresh exact outputs from two interior expansions and the boundary, projection, and graph calculations. The interior implementations agree on every key, signed coefficient, magnitude assignment, rounded bound, and aggregate. The retained provenance records describe the second implementation as separately authored from the mathematical specification and frozen before comparison with the first engine’s output. That specification already contained the claimed aggregate totals. These checks verify finite arithmetic; correspondence between formulas and code, and the analytic argument, require mathematical review.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The product cutoff on a suspended component. The horizontal variable is the suspension angle in $Φ(t,y) = (sint y,cost)$; the two end slices collapse to the tips $p_{+}$ and $p_{−}$. The vertical variable records the value of $w_{o}(y)$, not a coordinate on $Ω_{o}$. Gray regions have $ξ_{j} = 0$, and the blue region has $ξ_{j} = 1$; the intervening bands contain the smooth transitions. The scales are schematic, and no shape or regularity of the Hessian zero set is represented.",
      "figure_id": "fig:suspension-cutoff",
      "pdf_page": 32,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:suspension-cutoff",
          "exact_quote": "The product cutoff on a suspended component.  The horizontal\nvariable is the suspension angle in\n$\\Phi(t,y)=(\\sin t\\,y,\\cos t)$; the two end slices collapse to the\ntips $p_+$ and $p_-$.  The vertical variable records the value of\n$w_o(y)$, ",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,font=\\small]\n  \\fill[gray!24] (0,0) rectangle (11.4,.55);\n  \\fill[gray!24] (0,.55) rectangle (.7,3.15);\n  \\fill[gray!24] (10.7,.55) rectangle (11.4,3.15);\n  \\fill[blue!9] (1.45,1.10) rectangle (9.95,3.15);\n  \\draw (0,0) rectangle (11.4,3.15);\n  \\draw[densely dashed,gray] (.7,0)--(.7,3.15);\n  \\draw[densely dashed,gray] (10.7,0)--(10.7,3.15);\n  \\draw[densely dashed,gray] (0,.55)--(11.4,.55);\n  \\draw[dashed,blue!60!black] (1.45,0)--(1.45,3.15);\n  \\draw[dashed,blue!60!black] (9.95,0)--(9.95,3.15);\n  \\draw[dashed,blue!60!black] (0,1.10)--(11.4,1.10);\n  \\node at (5.7,2.05) {$\\xi_j=1$};\n  \\node[align=center] at (5.7,2.65)\n    {$w_o(y)\\ge 2/j,\\quad \\sin t\\ge 2/j$};\n  \\node at (5.7,.26) {$\\xi_j=0$ near the Hessian zero set};\n  \\node[rotate=90] at (.34,1.9) {$\\xi_j=0$};\n  \\node[rotate=90] at (11.06,1.9) {$\\xi_j=0$};\n  \\draw[->] (0,0)--(11.85,0) node[right] {$t$};\n  \\draw[->] (0,0)--(0,3.55) node[above] {$w_o(y)$};\n  \\node[below] at (0,0) {$0$};\n  \\node[below] at (11.4,0) {$\\pi$};\n  \\node[left] at (0,.55) {$1/j$};\n  \\node[left] at (0,1.10) {$2/j$};\n  \\node[below,align=center] at (0,-.52) {$p_+$};\n  \\node[below,align=center] at (11.4,-.52) {$p_-$};\n  \\draw[<-] (.7,-.04)--(.7,-.72)--(2.35,-.72)\n    node[right] {$t=\\arcsin(1/j)$};\n  \\draw[<-] (9.95,-.04)--(9.95,-1.23)--(8.65,-1.23)\n    node[left] {$t=\\pi-\\arcsin(2/j)$};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Schematic diagram showing the product cutoff domain in coordinates $(t, w_o(y))$. The horizontal axis is labeled $t$ with ticks at $0$ (labeled $p_+$ below) and $\\pi$ (labeled $p_-$ below). The vertical axis is labeled $w_o(y)$ with ticks at $0$, $1/j$, and $2/j$. Left vertical strip from $t=0$ to $t = \\arcsin(1/j)$ is shaded gray with label $\\xi_j = 0$. Right vertical strip from $t = \\pi - \\arcsin(2/j)$ to $\\pi$ is shaded gray with label $\\xi_j = 0$. Bottom horizontal strip from $w_o(y)=0$ to $1/j$ across all $t$ is shaded gray with label \"$\\xi_j = 0$ near the Hessian zero set\". Intermediate regions between values $1/j$ and $2/j$ contain dashed boundary lines representing transition bands. Central region where $w_o(y) \\ge 2/j$ and $\\sin t \\ge 2/j$ is shaded blue with label \"$\\xi_j = 1$\"."
    }
  ],
